' PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

"WIFE," said a henpecked husband, "go to bed." "I won't!" "Sit up, then. I will be obeyed somehow." "PUT me in the same room" (says an old philo- sopher) "with a number of young girls, and I will tell you, when the postman knocks—merely by watching the looks of some, and the actions of others—how many of them are in love."

"CAPITAL" CHAT.

The flatness of things political is equally shown by the undue attention to the words ot Sir William liarcoutt hinting that this might be his last, Budget. All public men have failings, and his best triend will not say that this particular form ot words is not a failing of Sir William Harcourt which runs some risk of becoming habitual. No one behind the scenes is seriously disquieted. There is the most absolute confidence in Sir William Harcourt' s good sense as well as in his strength and power sea leader. Everyone who knows him is aware that his true motto for his political action is the well-remembered pledge at the Foreign Office meeting last year, that whatever post is assigned him he " will stand by the ship. His leadership is more and more appreciated month by month, for the very obvious reason that he has shown tact as wed as astuteness in steering the Government programme, and that be knows eminently well what not to say as well as what to say.

I was at Bow-street on Monday, to witness the first appearance of Jabez Balfour in the dock. ihere was a long delay before the case was pro- ceeded with, owing to the Treasury having to arrange the charges upon which they are to prose- cute, and .Balfour was not in the end more than an hour in court altogether. Be was dressed in blue, the coat looking as if had been newly taken from a trunk into which it had been packed with no great neatness. Be looked florid and well, and not so stout as of old. His air of assurance was most marked. He gazed around the court as if he owned , it, and began passing notes to his counsel as soon as he entered the dock. His beard is now grey and Btubbly.

The agenda paper for the International Congress of Miners, which is to commence its sittings in Paris on Monday, June 3, has just been issued. Until the election of officers has been completed the proceed- . ings of the Congress will be conducted under the presidency of Mr. T. Burt, M.P. No delegate will be permitted to attend the Congress Who is not a miner or a secretary- of a miners' association. Resolutions in favour of a legal eight-hour day will be submitted by the French representatives and by the Miners' Federation ot Great Britain. The Con- gress will also be incited to consider the Question of over-production of coal and what steps should be taken to prevent the same so as to regulate prices and wages." The Fr nch delegates will bring forward a resolution declaring in favour of making employers responsible for all accidents in mines except" where it can be proved that the person has committed suicide." Aleasures will be considered for improving the sanitary condition of the mines and for securing additional workingmen inspectors. The German delegates propose that all resolutions w sich are carried unanimmsly shall be sent by the secretaries of the Congress to the respective Gov- ernments of the countries represented, with a request that steps may be taken to give effect to them. It is expected that over a million of miners will be represented at the Congress.

The Universal Food alio Cookery Association is making rapid strides towards success in its efforts to popularise the culinary att. Its eighth annual Exhibition, opened at Portman Rooms on Tuesday by Prince Edward of Saxe-Weimar, presented many features of real interest. The first saloon was de- voted to the display of trophies designed for buffet use. One of these, a combination of salmon, shell- fish, and boiled rice solidified to represent marble, Inas a real triumph. This, of course, was the work ot a recognised authority on table decoration. But the amateur was also to the fore with innumerable delicacies for every imaginable course likely to fine a place in an English bill of fare. Visitors of the severely practical sort probably found their chief pleasure in watching the progress of the cooking competitions, which went busily fasrward behind a rampart of gas and stoves, the winner of the first prize being entitled to a scholarship at the National School for Cookery. Among the competitors were young girls from Church and Board schocls, work- ing side by side with young ladies in dainty kitchen blouses. Hard by were a number of army cooks making the best of the British soldier's -daily ra- tions according to the latest regulations of the Army School of Cookery. If our redcoats really get such good soup as that which the lookers-on were invited to taste, their lot is not altogether unenvi- able. " Marine" cookery was also worthily repre- sented ; and, last but not least, the. triumph of electricity as a culinary auxiliary was admirably exemplified.

The superior outspokenness and frankness gene- rally of the American Press is forcibly exemplified by tte following extract:—" Boston society is on wheels, as it were, so universal has become the cyc- ling fad in that set. . . Mrs. 'Phil' Ely, who is training down her weight very successfully and may still recover the fisore that used to make her famous, Mrs. 'Jake' Peabody, who is immensely pretty in her wheeling rig, Miss Stackpole and others, were of the number that went out. Mrs. Harry Burnham, who; by the way,is celebrated for her small and neat foot, is perhaps the most fetch- ing cn her wheel of the smart set that ride."

One of the most interesting experiments under- taken by the London 'County Council is that of abolisliinrr the middleman contractor and itself do- ing its own work. The experiment has now been on trial for twelve mouths, during which time sixty-two different undertakings have been carried to a successful completion. The estimated cost of these was £176.086 17s. 11d., and the actual cost has been .C 178,863 18s. GI Considering that the department has only been in existence for twelve months, this result is eminently satisfactory ; but the Moderates do not like it, and will .make an effort to have the arrangement dropped and the old method of employing contractors revived. They are not likely to be successful, since the majority of members believe the Works Department should have a longer trial at any rate.

A deputation representing the County Councilsj ol Lancashire and other localities had an interview with Mr. Gardner, Minister of Agriculture on Tues- day. The particular point urged upon the depart- ment was the necessity for taking, measures against what is known as" foul brood "in bees, but Mr. Snape, who with Sir John Gorst, Sir Mark Stuart, .and others took part in the proceedings, extended this into a request not merely for efforts for the Cure and prevention of a particular disease, but for the encouragement of that technical instruction 'in bee-keeping towards which the Lancashire County Council has already made grants. It was pointed out that the £lOO,OOO or £150,000 at present derived from the production of lloney is not one- tenth of what might be realised by an intelligent development of agriculture.

The Radical and Labour Commit tee has appointed a committee ot four to report as to the desirability of issuing to all the constituencies concerned a black list of the unofficiar Liberal members who voted for the Duke of Coburg's annuity. It was also agreed to draw up a list of questions to be sub- mitted to 'Radical candidates at elections failing satisfactory replies to which the support of the Radical Committee will be withheld.

O'Donovan Rossa,the well-known Irish American, was a visitor to the flou-e of Commonson Tuesday evening and for a time was a subject of much in- terest in the strangers'smoking-room. He was shown round the I oty-o hy Mr. James O'Connor, M.P., who was sentenced at the same time, and spent some years as his fellow prisoner.

It is stated that the Count and Countess de Castellano (née Gould) are now busily engaged in preparing the plans for a sumptuous residence in the Avenue du Bois de Boulogne. A large site has been secured at the corner of the Avenue Malakoff at the cost of 700 francs per metre. The American countess will now carry out the dream of bet life, and a considerable portion of Mr. Jay Goulds money will he spent in an exact reproduc- tion of the Grand Trianon of Versailles. surrounded by spacious gardens. M Samson is the architect Chosen to carry out this elaborate work.

I hear that the Liberal prospects in West Edin- burgh are exceedingly good. Since the la,; election when the present Earl of Sdborne defeated Mr. Buchanan.' much has happened." It is estimated that two hundred and ninety-three hairs on the head, thirty-nree on the chin, twenty-three on the forearm, and nineteen on the back of the hand are respectively contained in an area of one quarter or an inch. Thumb rings are very common in the East. These are very often made of precious metal, ivory, jet and precious stenes, but generally they are of fine jade. The cavity is not cylindrical, but swells out at; the base and middle. This enables the Owner to wear it lower down, and also prevents it slipping. LADIES' LETTER.

MT DEAREST NELLIE,

I dare say you have noticed how prevalent white is becoming on all our garments. Not only do we see white facings and white linings, but the smartest of blouses are made of white muslin and lace and chiffon, while serge and tweed dresses have large muslin collars turned back upon them. Nothing is prettier than this touch of white on dark dresses. I have seen a charming blue serge dress this week, with a triple collar of cream- coloured lawn, edged with lace, turned down round the neck, and the front a marvel of soft muslin and cream-coloured lace. Then, again, I have met a black alpaca gown with a front of white satin striped with pale yellow insertion, and a large collar of white satin turned down on the coat, with an applique of lace patterns all over it. I was never more delighted with anything than I am with some of this year's spring costumes. Those large revers and immense sleeves are, I think, awfully becoming. One of the very prettiest cos- tumes I have seen was made of the newest fashion- able alpaca. The immense revers, the cuffs, and the collars were trimmed with Guipure lace. The skirt was just full enough to be becoming without too closely approaching the crinoline madness. The corsage a basque, with its trimming, looked very charming. One of the pretty picture hats would look very becoming with this costume.

Tho spring wraps shown this year are more charming, IL think, than for several seasons. The preference is given to capes, which are very short, only a few long ones being seen, which is proof con- clusive that the long cape is a thing of the past, for this season at least. The capes are plain, the trimming being reserved for the collar only, which is abundantly covered with ribbon, lace, and in some instances buckles. Some have merely a plain velvet collar, thus making the entire cape plain. Some have a double cape. This craze for short capes is a sensible freak of fashion, after all, for when one considers it they are the more durable cape, and wear and look better. The long cape is always getting caught in doors, and sat down on, and twisted around the arms. Such usage must pull any material out of shape. The short cape, on the other hand, is out of harm's way on any of these lines, and is always trim, natty, and stylish. A pretty and inexpensive blouse can be made in shot rose and fawn surah silk, spotted. The sleeves should be very full. The yoke, which is circular, is trimmed with two bands of tiny gathers, which almost resemble a necklet, and the collar band is frilled at the sides, fastening, of course, at the back. A pretty little bow at the

waist adds greatly the appearance. By the way, while speaking of necklets, I ought to remind you that ruffles are certain to be worn by everyone soon.

We are always having some novelties in straw, and the latest appears to have a curious felt-like surface. It is soft to the eye and to the touch, and is made in the palest and the brightest shades of cerise. It is eminently becoming when trimmed with black feathers and black jetted butterflies' wings, this latter being a very popular form of trimming, and a decidedly pleasing variety from the floral decorations, with which we have perhaps been a little surfeited lately. The most exquisite of ribbons are being used on the hats just now;. while, as no hat or bonnet is eomplote without something setting up erect upon it, a group of five or six green lily leaves buckled at the 'base with diamonds must be voted among one of the most attractive novelties. Imagine this decorating the centre of the front of a hat of a shade of light green straw, which is ornamented on one side with a bunch of Parma violets, and on the other with a bunch of pale yellow roses, while at the back arranged to rest on the hair is a large how of pale- green and yellow shot ribbon. The effect is de- lightful. But the green straws are not so new as the pink straws, which are, however, rather smart and showy for everyday occasions, but which undoubtedly look delicious when trimmed, as I have suggested, with black. They are fit to grace festivities when decked with shaded pink roses and shot pink ribbon. Unquestionably, though, they are more hem ning to dark than to fair people. Most of the picture hats, too, are

very pretty. They vary'a great deal in size. •we of those that pleased me most had the brim made of net, with lace edge outlined with fancy straw, and the new high crown, trimmed with shot ribbon, chiffon, osprey, and shaded roses. • Yours lovingly, JszonE.

Rickets.

Rickets is unfortunately a very common disease amongst children, and is due to parents feeding their children improperly. It is quite preventable. A child that shows as mntoms of rickets wants nourishing t make blood ; milk and strong beef tea should be the main articles of diet. Children usuAllv develop rickets between the time they are seven months and twenty- four months old. It is very iloport4l t parents should he careful in feeding their children, because the disease leads sometimes ti such dreadful uistertions. %ekes is not a disease peculiar to human beings, but is also readily contracted by young animals, whose food is perhaps given theM by man. rhe young lions at the, Zoo, not so very bang ago, got rickets through being fed on horse flesh, which did not supply them with the phosphate 61 lime they wanted for their own bones. !Children should always be fed at regular intervals, whether they have a tendency to rickets or no, and should not be allowed to eat trash between their meals and so destroy their appetite fir proper nourishing and and valual le food. Farinaceous food should only be given sparingly to children until they have Both lent teeth to champ it in their mouth. Tea should never be given and alcoholic drinks entirely withheld. Next to good and whelesome food, fresh air is of the greatest importance, and a rickety child sh.uld be taken for its daily airing without fail.

What Shall We Eat ?

Most people are..b.;:his--t-i-me.ti.rWly-prepared with the knowledge that ne ciief ust7s of food are to form the material of the body and repair its wistes ; to yield heat to keep the body warm and to provide muscular awl ,ttlf-r power for the w-rk it has to do. Dr. W. 0, At- water has prepared two tables showing, first, the com- position of food materi-ls, the most important of which are the nutritive ingredients and their fuel value ; second, the pecuniary economy of fond, in which the amount of nutrients is stated in pounds. In the firt table we find that but er has the greatest fuel value, fat pork coming second, and the balance of the foods being rated as fuel in the following order :—Cheese, oatmeat, sugar, rice, beans, cornmeal, wheat ft mr, wheat bread, leg of mutton and beef sirloin, round of beef, mackerer. salmon. Codfish, msters, cow's milk, and potatoes stand ver; low as fuel hinds. The same authority tells s that a quart of milk,thrre- ! quarters of a pound of moderately fat beef, and five ounces of Wheat flour,all contain about the same amount of nutritive material ; hut we pay different prices fir I them and they have different values for nutriment. Thc milk comes nearest to being a perfect food. It contains all of the different knds of nutritive materials that the body needs. Bread made from the wheat flour will sup- port life. It con'ains all of the necessary ingredients frit nourishment,but not in the proportions best adapted for ordinary use. A man might live on beef alone, but it would he a very one-sided and imperfect diet. Hut meat and bread together make the essentials of a healthy diet. Such are the facts of experience. The advancing science of later years explains them. This explanati,.n takes into account, not simply quantities of meat and bread and milk and Other materials which we eat, but also,the nutritive ingredients or •mutrients' which they contain.

THE EX

. 1895.

WE PARTED AT THE ALTAR; BY LAURA JEAN LIBBEY, Author of "A Mad Betrothal," "lone," "Parted by Fate," "Floribel s Lover," etc., etc.

CHAPTER XXV. COLD, LOVELESS AND DREARY. FOR one instant Karl Lancaster stood quite still like one stricken dumb. Doris was gone! Gone! How the words smote upon his ear! "Why, how white you look, Mr. leincaster !" said Mrs. Morgan, looking curiously into the young man's white, handsome face. He turned abruptly away and flung open the door. Mrs. Morgan was at his side in an iustant, laying a detaining hand on his arm "What are you about to do, Mr. Lancaster!" she cried, anxiously. "I am going to search for her until I find her," answered Karl, ,hoarsely. "I will search the whole world over for her, if needs be." And with these words he rushed from the room and out of the house.

" Poor fellow," sighed Mrs. Morgan. "It was well that I sent the girl away before he returned. Why, he would actually marry that girl if he could find her. And, oh! what a scene there would be under the old doctor's roof! I am sure his father would disinherit him on the spot. He would never forgive him. Never !" Meanwhile Karl Lancaster, with his heart on fire and his brain in a whirl, was threading his way swiftly along the crowded thoroughfare. Which way had poor Doris gone? Where should he find her? Suddenly a thought occurred to him that almost took his breath away. He remembered Doris had been on her way to an employment agency on that dly the accident had occurred which had so nearly proven fatal. What more natural or probable than that she had bent her steps in that direction now? He remembered the number, and lost no time in making his way there at once. And the first person whom his eyes rested upon as he opened the door was Doris her- self. _

She started in surprise as she saw him. What had he followed her here? And, like Mrs. Morgan, she was struck by the strange pallor of his face. "Doris—Miss Brandon 1" he said, coming up to her and catching one of he little hands that lay so idly in her lap. "Thank God, I have found you!' "I have much that is of the greatest importance to say to you, Doris," he went on, "but I cannot speak here." The reception-room beyond was almost deserted at this hour of the day. Karl pointed toward it. "Come there with me, Doris," he said ; "I have that to tell you which will sound more like the pages of romance than reality,. but it only serves to illustrate the old adage that truth is indeed stranger than fiction." Doris shrank back from him, with the memory of that scene, in which he had madly declared his love for her, beseeching her to marry him, rushing over her mind. _

Had he followed her to repeat that scene? she wondered.

Karl saw her hesitancy, and read her thoughts and dread aright. • "It is not of myself I wish to speak—not one word," he said, flus mg deeply. "That which I' have to say concerns you, and you alone, Doris. Come !'

In wonder and dismay, Doris followed him to the reception-room beyond. He placed a chair for her, and stood leaning against the mantel, seem- ingly in no hurry to begin. Doris sat twisting her hands nervously together, wondering what new calamity was about to befall her now. _ _

"It was fate that led you to me, Doris," began Karl Lancaster, iii skily. "But before I p:oceed, I must be sure that I am on the right track— beyond any possible shadow of a doubt ere I raise your hopes. "In the first place, are you the Doris Brandon whose life, up to a few weeks ago, was passed at Madame Delmar's seminary at Beech Grove ?" He carcely breathed in his intensity to catch her answer. _ .

"Yes, I am that most unhappy girl !" breathed Doris, faintly. "But why do you ask? How could you have found out that ?" Like one in a confused dram, Doris listened while he told her the wonderful story—of her father and mother's return, after long years, to reclaim their child, only to find her gone, none knew whither; of their grief, her mother's death, and the fatal accident which caused her father to follow her soon after, having barely time to make his will, leaving his vast wealth to his daughter, with the earnest prayer she should be sought for, night and day, until she was found. This fortune was to be kept in trust for Mr. Hulbert Brandon Fielding's daughter until she is eighteen. My father has been searching for the lost heiress ever sinue.''_

"I thought when I heard the name, 'Doris Brandon,' it had a strangely familiar sound; but not until to-day--an hour since—did the astound- ing revelation cs.cur to me that the young gbl fate ' had drifted across my path, and the lost heiress my father was searching for, were one and the. same. Allow me to be the first, Miss Fielding, to congratulate you upon the good fortune that has befallen you." The beautiful, dreamy, blue eyes were expanded in the greatest amazement. All the colour had faded from the fair young face, leaving it paler than the petals of a white lily. He believed she had not comprehended what lie had said, and he repeated: "You are an he:ress, Miss Fielding—heiress to a million of dollars in your own r ght, which my father, as your guardian, is to hold in trust for you until you are eighteen." "Oh, Mr. Lancaster, do you really believe it ?" cried Doris, breathlessly "Is there no mistake? Surely there must be. Fate has been so unkind to me all my life it must bc playing some trick on me now," she declared. "If you are the Doris Brandon who has lived with Madame Delmar up to a short time ago, there can be no possible mistake." Doris sprang to her feet, trembling with emotion. "I can scarcely believe that I, Doris Brandon, whom the world has used so cruelly, am indeed an heiress," she cried, pushing back the rings of golden curls from her flushed face in a bewildered sort of way. "lam only a poor dependent, whom

nobody has ever cared for," Sim went on, piteously. "I have never had any money. lam unused to wealth am! luxury. My life has been hard, lone and dreary. I should not know what to do with money." " After you have seen a little more of the world, you won't say that,' smiled Karl. " Why most ladies know how to make money fairly fly. lam

not surprised at your agitation. A aisciosure such as I have made to you would turn almost any young lady s head. No wonder you are be- wiidered. Perhaps no young girl ever had more cause. But you must bear prosperity as you have borne adversity. From this time out your whole life will be changed. Your future will be brilliant ; for, with health and beauty combined, life is full of golden promise." A dazzling smile parted the girl's beautiful crim- son lips, and the colour surged back- to the sweet face in a burning tide. In her great excitement, she forgot what Karl was saying to her. She did not hear one word. Doris's thoughts had drifted back t Frederick Thornton, and her ;lout was crying out: " Would he have turned from me had he known I was one day to become a great heiress— quite as much an heiress as Vivian, whom he loved ?" "Oh, Frederick," she moaned out in the bitterness of her Own heart, " what would all the wealth in the world be worth to me without your love?" Doris offered no resistance when Karl proposed that she should accompany him home at once. Dr. Lancaster was more than amazed when, a little late a cab stopped before his palatial resi. dence, and from i stepped his son, leading by the hand a young girl, plainly dressed, almost to shabbiness, but with a face as gloriously beautiful as an angel's. It had been the one secret fear of his life that some day his son might marry on the impulse of the moment, attracted by the p etty face of Anne lovely working•girl chance might throw in his way. And he meant that this handsome son of his should marry an heiress, or not marry at all. Like his wife, he had the secr-t hope that the heiress, Miss Fielding, might be found. and that she anti his son might be mutually attracted toward each other. His brow grew dark as the quick footsteps drew near the library.. As the door opened, he rode to his feet, coldly confronting the two who stood on the threshold. He expected to bear tbe words : " tis ao-i‘tt,ut.‘t, 1.4.xe iJi‘tig.llL i+4

.

home a daughter. Welcome my bride, father." Instead, his son stepped forward, leading the pretty, timid, shrinking young girl by the hand, saying, hurriedly : "Father, behold Miss Doris Brandon Fielding ! I have discovered the long-lost heiress—at last ! B,cl her welcome, father." " What ! " cried the old gentleman, quite believing his ears had deceived him,—" do I hear aright ?' "I hope so father," said Karl, smiling. "I said I had found Miss Fielding, for whom you have been searching so long and patiently. Bid her welcome." _ _

" I—do—not—comprehend," exclaimed the old gentleman. "How and where did you meet this young lady, and what reason have you for believing her to be the child of my poor friend Fielding and his heart-b oken wife ?" _ _ . .

"Her face—in which the resemblance to both is so strongly marked—might answer that question— even if I did not," laughed Karl, and in a few brief words he explained to his father, and his mother also, who had been summoned, the story of his meeting with Doris. Then there was no lack of warmth in the doctor's hearty greeting. _

"Why, it sounds just like a romance, my dear." declared Mrs. Lancaster, taking tha lovely young orphan in her arms and kissing her delightedly. " Welcome—a thousand times welcome—Doris ! You will permit me to call you that, will you not? It does sound so cold and formal to say Miss Fielding, and you are to be one of the family, for " at least a year yet, dear,. until my husband's guardianship of you expires." "Call me Doris, by all means," the girl replied "I should like that best. I—am afraid I can never get used to being addressed as Miss Feild- ing ; even now, I am afraid I shall wake up and find all this but a dream," said Doris, piteously.

CHAPTER XXVI.

DORIS IN LUXURY. THIC sudden change from earning her own living to the luxury in which Doris found hersslf, would have dazzled almost any young girl, older and wiser than Doris, and she wondered how she had ever lived through that cold, dark past,—that desolate past, which was left behind her for ever- more. Mrs. Lancaster made all possible haste in attend- ing to Doris's wardrobe. "You must discard that blue merino dress of yours at once, my dear. The neighbours would never forget it if they saw you in that," declared the lady. So the next fortnight was spent in daily excur- sbrp.s to the establishments of the leading dress- makers. Doris could scarcely realise that the radiant little figure which her mirror reflected, robed in shim- mering silk,—in whose tiny, shell-like ears, and on whose small white hands costly diamonds glittered like stars, was the same Doris who had sobbed her- self to sleep, many and many a night, with Madame Delmar's cruel, taunting words ringing in her ears: _ _ _

"You are a miserable, dependent creature; you ought to be thankful for a crust of bread, and a roof to cover your head, —content with my cast-off dresses, and that you have been given any educa- tion whatever." Now all the world was changed for Doris. She was courted, petted, and flattered; a widely differ- ent personage from the timid young girl we first introduced to our readers. •,

.—.--- • "I am proud of you, Doris," said Mrs. Lancas- ter, complacently, one day. "Your beauty will create quite a commotion in society ; you will turn the heads of all the marriageable young fel- lows. Young girls of your age generally have some ideal hero pictured in their minds. But when your Prince Charming comes along, you must not be in a hurry to marry. A year or so will be plenty of time for that." "I shall never marry," said Doris, paling to the very lips, and a look of keen distress in her dark blue eyes. "Please do not mention marriage to me again, Mrs. Lancaster. I shall never marry.') "Fie, fie, my dear. No one but old maids whom nobody wants, or someone who has been crossed in love, ever makes such a remark as that. It sounds ridiculous on the lips of a pretty girl. Yin' cannot rule your own heart, my dear Doris. Love goes where Reaven intended it should go, and when the right one comes along you will say to yourself, "I can never be happy without him, and it will end as it should—Lin a marriage." But Doris shook her curly head with a dreary

sigh. That night Dr. Lancaster and his wife held quite a lengthy debate in their own apartments in reference to Doris, and, as usual, the determined little lady carried the point. "Doiis is pretty I grant—but she lacks the culture that fits one for society," declared Mrs. Lancaster, energetically, "and it must be attended to without delay. Her education in music and the higher branches has been sadly neglected, We mu-t engage competent persons to remedy this defect at once." And so they settled the matter. Doris was an apt pupil, and progressed wonderfully with her studies. There was one , incentive above all others that urged Doris on in her dealt° to fit herself for the social world that lay before her— she would be pure to meet Frederick Thornton there; that was the beginning and the end, the goal of her ambition. She told herself she must see him just once ; then she would be willing to shut herself away from the gay world for ever after that one hour of triumph. N. timid young girl likes to discuss those vague, sweet heart-thoughts to a matter of fact old lady, and Doris shrank irom even mentioning that piti- ful, broken love-dream to practical Mrs. Lancas- ter. Remembering Madame Delmar had declared Doris had eloped from the seminary, Mrs. Lancas- ter broached very carefully whether or not Doris had ever had a lover, and how she happened to leave the seminary to seek a position of governess in New York. "I will speak of this matter just once," Doris replied, with a little sob in her voice. "I do not like to think of it. It is like opening an old wound. Madame Delmar turned the from the seminary door, because she was tired of keeping the penniless girt—who was dependent on her bounty—under tier roof longer. I was forced to go out into the world. And as for a lover—air, Mrs. Lancaster, I had no lover. No one loved sue; no one.'' . .

The time came at last when Miss Fielding, Doctor Lancaster's lovely ward, was finally • launched upon the tidal wave of society. As Mrs. Lancas- ter had foreseen, she created quite a furore. Receptions, par ice, balls, followed each other in rapid succession, and pretty Doris was queen of them a!l. Yo; she always came home .with the same piteous 'ono in her lovely, childish eyes. It bad been another evening lost out of her life, for she 1i4,1 not met Fiederick Thornton. Believing her thad, had he married Vivian and taken her abroad? From the moment this idea occurred to her, Loris had it feverish desire to go abre Au 000rtunity soon presented itself. Dr. Lan- caster received a letter one day, urging his imme- dia•e presence in London, and it was arranged that his wife and Doris should accompany him on this flying trip. The next steamer brought Karl. " I thought lather might need me, perhaps," he said ; but his mother !mew better. Site was sure Doris's brie •slesh 1 I the As time rolled on, Mrs. Lancaster began to grow anxious over her ; his deep adoring love for Doris was so marked, while she in return, gave him only a sister's kindly friendship— nothing more.

All in vain pretty young girls gazed at the tall, handsome, fair-haired young American • he had no eyes for them; his whole thought, night and day, was of Doris—only of her. "My patient love, my great devotion, must win love in return. I can wait for it," he toid himself

over and over again. Three long years passed, and it seemed to Karl Lancaster he was further than ever from Doris's love. If ever a woman had been born without a heart, or it. had turned to marble in her breast, that woman was Doris.

The three years spent abroad had done much for Doris ; it ),a(1 expanded her from the bud of girl- hood into the hloorn of perfect womanhood. She bad been pretty as the child of seventeen. At twenty, taller by almost half a head, she had grown into the full, dazzling beauty of perfect womanhood. Suitors sought her hand, laying love, fame, and fortune at her feet ; hti t to one and all she gave the Same gracious answer: " I thank you for the kind compliment you pay me in at-ling me to be your wife. lam sorry you love me. 1 sit .11 never mary—,ever." When Mrs. Lancaster saw Doris dismiss one lover after another, a StrilAge smile came to her 11PS.

In reply toa question of the MAYOR, the Borough Sur- veyor said the sewer from the old harracks WPfir across Ow street and down Slutchers-lane to the outtall in the river.

But if they were to have an abattoir let them take the two questions together. Councillor MONIES said that if Councillor Roberts' sug- gestion was adopted, it would settle the matter for the present, but if they had to vote between Queen-st. and the militia stores for the pig market he should certainly vote for the militia stores because he considered it far the best position of the two. What had been said seemed rather to turn on the question of drainage. O the two sites suggested Wilson Patten-sr. had the a tvatitage, be- cause there they had a drain which was comparatively short, going direet from the old barracks down Slut- cher's-lane and into the river, and into which the drain- age of no house would enter. At Queen-st. they had a low lying, level piece of land from which tha fall mu.,t necessarily be slight, and not only !hat, but this foul matter weuld have so much longer distance to travel through the sewers than it would from the old barracks. , As regarded the pig market spoiling the neighbourhood, personally he was not a•raid, and he had recently taken a house in Warmarleigh-at. notwithstanding the probe- bilk! of the militia stores being converted into a swine and Cattle market—(hear, hear)—anti he believed the Chairman of the Finance Committee was already resi- dent in that street. _

Councillor Woons was of opinion that if the other side had taken up the question, an equal number of naMe3 could have been obtained in favour of the removal ot the market to the stores. Alderman Francomh had pointed ut that there had never been an epidemic on Ag Hill, and if that was due to the pig market being there, he should think the people in Wils,m Patten-at. ought to welcome the proposal to take the pig market there in order that they might enjoy aaimilar immunity. (Laugh- ter.)

OAS COMMITTER.-CLOSING OF MERSZY-STREET OAS WORKS

_ Councillor R. T. FAIRCLOUGH in moving the ad.mtion of the minutes which appeared in our last issue said he wished the Council to notice that the policy of entirely removing the Mersey-street works to Longford had been considered desirable for years by Mr. Haddock and the Gas Committee, but to be sure that it would be advan- tageous the committee instructed Mr. Thomas New- bigging, an eminent consulting as engineer, to inspect and report upon their removal. His report, which was dated April 13-h, 1892, was sent at that time to each member of the Council,and as it corroborated the views of the Committee the gas engineer was instructed not to spend any money upon the works only what should absolutely be required to keep them efficient. They had, however. now come to that stage that they must either spend a considerable amount of money upon repairing the purifiers at Mersey-street or remove altogether to Longford. In Mr. Newtligging's report the quantity of coal-delivered at Mersey-st. was stated at 3.750 tons, and as the basis of his report was calculated at that quantity the loss of working at Mersey-street in comparison with Longford was £66 for that year after allowing £13,320 to build extensions at Longford at 3i. per cent. interest. But Mr. Newbigging finished his report by the following sentence :--0 Although the saving shown is but trifling, the prospective advantages are such that the project is worth carrying into effect." Now the quantity of coal used this last winter was 5 704 tons and taking the same basis as contained in Mr. Newhiggines report the less of working at Mersey-street bad been 505 after allowing £13.320 to build extensions at Longford at 3 per cent. interest. The gas engineer advised and the Gas Com- mittee therofore thought that'as the sale isf gas increased, the greater the loss would he by staying at Mersey-street as shown by the basis of Mr. Newhigging and what had been done this last winter. They could if desired remove to Longtord and be ready to give up possessim of the works to the Sanitary Works Committee in twelve months if the Council would sanction the removal, and he understood the Sanitary Werks Committee were quite willing to take over the Mersey-street .works at Mr. Nembigging'n valuation. Councillc r llsrersan lenondod the motion, endorsing all that had been said by Councillor Fairclough, the chairman of the committee. Ever since he (Cotracillor flepherd) had had any connection with the undertaking he had been convinced of the necessity for closing the works in Mersey-st., and removing to Longford. Of the wisdom of that policy ho was still strongly convinced.

Councillor ROBERTS, for the purpose ofspeaking a few words moved an amendment. Pressing the Chairman of the Committee to give an utsdertaking to the Council that he would not deal with the gas profits without first consulting the Council as to the direction in which they should he dealt with. He contended that the Commit- tee should reduce the price of gas by 3d. per 1.000 feet. It appeared that the profit-a on the gas undertaking had ieeti £2,326 lbs. 211 Of this sum the Committee had alraady disposed of £656 lls. 11d, in making an ablte- meat in respect of the charge for street lamps. The remainder, £1,670 3s 314., still remained to be dealt with.

Alderman BOLTON seconded the amendment. Councillor LONGTON supported the confirmation of the minutes, saying that the proposal which the nomminee had made had received their careful and serious con- sideration. GOOD-BYE FOR EVER.

A DOCTOR'S ADVICE TO A DESPAIRINU WOMAN.

FROM THE " BURNLEY GAZETTE."

Burnley gossip ba4 been busy concerning the happy release of Miss E oily P uchar, of 96, Abel-street, Burn- ley, and her story will be read with interest. The " Burnley G a-qte " inter eiewed Miss Poucher,who said, "I have sufferre I terribly from indigestion. I was brok n down for over four yea re. Sometimes I would ;nand a little, but only for a few days. During the whole four years I could do very little, but I was re- stored to perfect health by the use of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People." " How did it all begin? "

"I started with biliousness,sick headache, and general weakness. I kept up a pretty fair app4ite,but my food never seemed to do roe any go )d. Ex9rtion of any sort felt awful drudgery, and I got thormghly worn out and weary. Night after night I was unable to get a wink of sleep, and our folk had to wrap in,. in hot b lankets, because of the cramp in my limbs. I had four different doctors, out I 4ot so bad that I lest all interest in life, and longed to fall asleep and wake no more. I went to Lincolnshire to see what country air would do. I hadn't been there many days when I felt worse, and decidscl to come borne. I thought it was all over, and that I had better die at home. Before I left I had some talk with the village doctor. He noticed how ill I was, and we had a chat about my case. He said the best thing I could do was to try Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. When I reached home I told our folks what the doctor had said and it seems someone else had recommended them to mother while I was away. So we decided to try them. Soon I began ;•.o feel decidedly better. I kept on taking them, and before the second 'boa was finished I was better than I had been for five years. This was less than a fortnight alter taking the first dose. People were sur- prised to see 1120 for they,all thought they had bid me "good-bye" for ever. I improved every week, and have not had a day's illness since. In fact I feel years younger than I did four years since." "And do you as cribe all this change to Dr. Williams, Pink Pills 1,"

"Certainly. I have talon nothing else since I started with them. They have made a different girl of me altogether.

The above are the facts written down as given, will- ingly and gratefully, by Miss Poucher and ty-r mother. A KNOWING PLANT.

The Ceylon Mimosa is said to be so sensitive that if a person will fix his eyes on a special branch, and slowly approach it, the plant is seen gradually to " and shrink within itself, as it wore, before it is touched by the observer's hand. Some little time ago, M. Baton was at Cereto. in the Island of Cuba, where he was the guest of an English coffee-planter, While sitting with the family on the broad piazza, which formed the front of the bungalow, a thrifty " S3nsitive plant" was recog- nised, and made the subject of remark. The doctor called his daughter of eleven sears from the house. "Lena " s-dd he " go and kiss the Mimosa." The child did so, laughing gleefully, and came away. The plant gave no total of shrinking from contact with the pretty child ! " Now," said the host, to M. Mon, " will you touch the plant 2" Rising to do so, he approached it with one hand extended, and before it had fairly come in contact, the nearest spray and leaves " wilted" visibly. " The plant knows the child," said the planter, " but you are a stranger." It was a puzz;ing experience which seemed to endow the Mimosa with intelligence.

IMPERIAL PA R.LIAMEN

MONDAY.

In the House of Commons on Monday evening, on the motion to go into Committ,e on the Welsh Church Bill, Mr. AsouriH said he had a command from the QUEEN to signify to the HOLM-, that her M±sty had been pleased to place at till disposal of Parliament for the purposes of the Bill her interest in bishoprics and other ecclesiastical dignitie. and benefices in Wales and Monmouthshire. The SPEAKER ruled out of order a nuinberof the motions for " instructions "to the Committee. Mr. GRIFFITH- BOSCAWEN moved that it shonld be an instruction to the Committee that they had power to divide the Bill into two Bills,one dealing, with the disestahlishment and the other with disendowments, and that the first Bill should he reported to the House before the other was proceeded with. Lord CRANBONE seconded the motion. Mr. AsQuits said the speeches of the mover and seconder of the motion ignored the fact that the House by a very decided majority on the second reading expressed its assent as well to etsestablishment. Sir M. Limas- BEACH and Sir R. WEBSTER spike in support of the motion, which on a division was rejected by 231 to 198. The House then went into Committee on the Bill. Various other amendments were rejected by majorities of 44 to 31.

The order for the second reading of the Local Veto Bill ci as postponed for a fortnight.

In the Rouse of Lords Lord KNUTSFORD wkerl for some information as to the recent annexation of territory north of Zululand. Lord RIPON ,ta.ted that the territory in question had always been acknowledged to be within the sphere of British influence. The Government would however, probably have abstained from annexing it if the South African Republic had prevented the citizens of the Republic from interfering ; but as they had faill to do sq, notwithstanding the remonstrances of Sir H. LOCH, there was no alternative but to annex the terri- tory. In taking that sten the Government were not animated by any unfriendly feeling towards the South African Republic, with whom they desired to maintain friendly relations.

===== 0002601_18950511_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
TUESDAY.

In thP House of Corn-in-o—rWo-ii Tuesday evening Sir W. HARCOURT, replying to a question, said he was not in a position to state when the second rea 'Mg of the Local Veto Bill would be taken. There was no truth in state- ments that the Government did not intend to proceed further with the Bill.

- The House again went into Committee on the Welsh Disestablishment Bill. On clause 1 Mr. MORE moved an amendment to accept from the operation of the measure that part of Montgomeryshire which is situated in the diocese of Hereford. The amendment was rejected by 234 to 206. Mr RANKIN moved an amendment to ex- cept from the Bill those portions of Radnorshire which were included in the diocese o Hereford. On a division the numbers were—for the amendment, 2)9 ; against, 237 ; majority against, 28, Mr. STUART-WORTLEY said th • clerks at the " Aye "lobby did not wait till he and Mr. TALBOT had passed through the gate, and therefore the maj )rity appeared to be two greater than it should be. The Chairman (Mr. MELLOR) directed that the names of Mr. Stuart-Wortley and Mr. Talbot should be added to the names of those who voted for the amendment, and the majority was thus reduced by two. Colooel KENYON-SLANEY (far Mr. KENYON) moved an amendment to exclude the detached portion of the county of Flint known as the Hundred of Mielor. Mr. J. H. LERIS strongly opposed the amendment, and Mr. Ascsurnt said the G wernment could not deal piecemeal with the par- ticular feelings of particular districts. The amendment was defeated by 191 to 168. Mr. GRIFFITH-BOSCAWEN moved an amendment to exclude the county of Mm- mouth. A discussion as to which country Monmouth- shire belonged to gave Mr. G. RUSSELL an opportunity of referring playfully to Sir E. A. BARTLETT'S patriotic spirit. ''The bon. member for the Eccieshall division had spoken of certain members as "non-English," and Mr. Rossett, mentioned that "nod" gave Brooklyn, U.S., as Sir ELLis's birthplace. Mr. ASQUITH, in opposing the amendment, said Monmouthshire had been ecclesiasti- cally a part of Wales from the earliest times. The ameedment was defeated by 201 to 176. The debate was then adj-urned to Monday next. The HOME SECRETARY'S Factories and Workshops Bill came before the Grand Committee on Trade on Tuesday; Mr. STUART-WORTLEY in the chair. On the question of artificial lighting Mr. Wutrinity obtained the exemption of " gassing "engines from the list of apparatus whose employment called for an increase of cubic space. A long discussion took place on a proposal hy Mr. ALLAN to secure the license annually " of every place where w -irk is done for an employer whose premises are else- where." This was intended to prevent the " sweating" of workpeople in unhealthy houses. Mr. Astra= as- sured the committee that the proposal of Mr. ALLAN would be inoperative,because it would thrown upon the inspecters work they could not possibly get through. He promised to bring up a clause dealing with sugges: tions that had been made. The proposal was rejected by 48 votes to eight. Au amendment by Sir C. DILKE making an employer who gave out work liable to heavy penalties if the work was done under insanitary condi- tions was defeated by 41 votes to nine, Mr. ASQUITH pointing out that the employer had no right of entry into the houses of his workpeople and could not be re- sponsible for their condition. The committee then ad- journed.

===== 0002601_18950511_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
WEDNESDAY.

In the House of Commons on Wednesday Mr. KNOX moved the second reading of a Bill to repeal the Irish I Crimes Act of 1887. He said the peaceable condition of Ireland was such as had not existed before within the 'memory of living man. Mr. J. O'Coloton seconded the motion. Mr. BARTON moved and Captain WCALMONT seconded the rejection of the Bill. Mr. MORLEY said that soon after he took office as Chief Secretary all the proclamations putting the Act in force were revoked, and the Government had not used the Act or any part of it. It was quite true that the Government were pleged to reveal the Crimes Act as soon as they could. But it was also true that they were pledged to bring in a Home Rule Bill, and they preferred to do that for which the country was tanking and leave the repeal of the Crimes Act for some future day. He did not think it possible to get this Bill through the House in a few days, but if a proposal was made to refer it to a Standing Commit- , tee he would not oppose it. He contended that the pr.). phecies uttered when he revoked the proclamations mak- ing the Act operative, that he had entered upon a fatal policy unworth of a British Minister, had not been borne out, lie regarded the Act as a tgrant invasion of the civil rights of Irishmen, and he had never voted with more perfect confidence than he was doing what the Constitution of the country demanded and what the welfare of all classes in Ireland needed than in voting for the second reading of this Bill. Sir E. CLARKE and Mr. T. W. Russet', spoke in opposition to the Bill. Mr. Bettroort said he would never be a party to throwing away so invaluable an instrument of liberty as the Crimes Act, although until the liberty of the subject was assailed he should he the last person to desire to use it. The Opposition, unlike the Covernment, could not shut their eves to the possibility that dark days might be in store for them. Just before half-past five the closure was nv,yed and was carried by 25 to 208, and the second reading of the Bill was passed by 222 to 203. A motion to refer the Bill to a Standing Committee was opposed, and had therefore to stand over.

THE CHANNEL ISLANDS AND MANC,HEiTER.

_ In connection with the service from Jersey to Man- chester, it is stated that throughout the new pot do season, which lasts for a month or six weeks, three steamers per week will leave St. Helier, Jersay, with potatoes for the Manchester market. The steamers cvill not take back cargoes, as the empty casks in which the potatoes will come will occupy all the available space on the outward trip. The vessels engaged in the trade will be the steamers RAPS Rock, Bishop Rock, and another, each or about 200 tons burden. rhis new departure is another of the enterprises of the Manchester, Bombay,. and General Navigation Company, and should the results prove as satisfactory as anticipated, further develop- ments of a similar character may be looked for at an any date. •

ENGLAND THE OGRE.

OURSELVES THROUO H FRENCH EYES.

Below will be found an article translated tro-in"" La Petrie "of the 25th of last msnth, which gives a fair idea of the latitude towards England and the English adopted by a certain section of the French Press. The writer is M. Lucien Millevoye, and " La Petrie " has a larger sale than any other evening papsr in Paris. Here is the article: —"Those who have not read the last num- bers ot the " Daily Telegraph " are quite ignorant of how capable is the English soul of pity and tenderness. The faTai of the Queen of Madagascar, so cruelly tor- ment& at this moment by the claims of France, compels irl our neighbours across the Channel the most heart- rending grief. They nobly conjure us not to abuse the superiority of our tortes, to spire a savage people, to listen to the generous voices which call to us, while there is yet time, for moderation and humanity. The Power which dares to address us, with such advice has itself acquired universal renown hr cold cruelty and relentless selfishness. The Anglo-Saxon race does not know how to pardon, nor forget; her re- sentments are ferocious, her memory pitiless. She reigns in suppressing, the vanquished. She establishes her dominions on pyramids of corpses. The English philanthropists who occupy themselves in so admirably a disinterested way about the anguish of these poor Hovas—will they deign t cast their eyes on ths door step of their own house ? They will 30^r suffer- ing capable Cf exciting their feelings • they will con- template wickedness terrible eneugl to trouble the equanimity of their patriotism. At Madagascar the mockery is a little too strong. England has commenced by ordering her missionaries to cause us every possible annoy rice;i and she has found ansongt these pious hypo- crites marvellous auxiliaries for the accomplishment of her designs. . . . The money for war voted. our vessels, our soldiers are ready. The !lovas can no more blind themselves to the storm which is about to break- over their heads. England exhorts them stiff to have confidence, and, does her best to animate their ceurage. English officers offer to instruct and command. them. France approaches. Her armada floats to Tamatave. The unhappy Queen feels the iron of the invader on her chest. She turns towards the English asking : "And your promise ? And your help ?'" She only gathers vain evidences of sympathy and compassion. England recedes at Madagascar before France, as she receded yesterday at the Nicer& ua before the United States, as she will recede to-morrow at Formosa before Japan,just as she will before all those who will have the courage to show her their teeth."

WARRINGTON WI RE-D RAWE RS. PRESENTATION AND CONCERT.

iavio

On Monday evening there was a large gathering in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street, of the Warrington wire-drawers and their friends. The occasion was the presentation of £lOO to Mr. E. Ansell, who as a wire- drawer has been totally incapacitated from work by paralysis of both arms, This sum of money was the proceeds of a 3d draw which was organised throughout the iron and steel wire-drawers of the country. lii con- junction with the presentation a concert had been got up by the following committee :—Messrs. Hardinen, Robin- son, Bullock, Rook, White. T. Ansell, T. Williams, A. I Owen, Jeffery Bennion, Haigh (chairman of committee). ,1 and S. Wettings (secretary). The chair at the concert I was taken by Mr. Pierparint., M.P., and he was accom- panied on the platform by Mr. T. Birchall, J.P., Mr. J. Whittle and Mr. S. Wellings. Before commencing, the CHAIRMAN expripsed his pleasure at being present on an occasion b 0 interesting mad touching as that for which were assembled that night. The following programme was then gone through, much to the enjoyment of the audience :.—Song," The White Squall," Mr. N. Cook ; song, " By the Fountain," Mrs. Pearson ; sang, " Vil- lage Blacksmith." Mr. E. Pownall ; recitation, " Lan- cashire dialect), Mr. C. Furness ; song, " Cur Jack," Mr. A. Griffiths; song, " Promise of Life," Miss Pem- berton ; comie song, "The Giddy Little Girl," Mr. W. Whittle ; song," Anchored," Mr.N. Conk •, song, " The Blue Alsatian Mountains," Mr. A. Griffiths ; sang. " When the Heart is Young," Miss Pemberton ; recita- tion (Lancashire dialect), Mr. C. Furnese ; seng," The Skipper," Mr. E. Pownall ; Comic song," Knocked him once," Mr. W. Whittle. The singers deserving of special mention were Mrs. Pearson. who gave a beautiful rendition of •' By the fountain," for which she was encored, and Mr Griffiths, whose singing of" Our Jack," met with an enthusiastic reception. Mr. W. Whittle as humorist gave unquali- fied amusement to the audience, and Mr. Furness by his well-delivered Lancashire reciations, contributed much to the success of the evening. During the interval the Chairman made the presenta- tion. and in the cour.e of his speech said that not only did " one touch of nature make the whole world kin, but one heavy blow of affliction made a man's mates his brothers. He was glad to know that the subserip- lion which had been gathered had not been made a pub- lic one throughout the town .but had been confined almost entirely to the iron and steel wiredrawere of Warrington, and in answering to the call be ventured to thank and tell them that they had done themselves and the town a very great credit. Referring to Mr. Ansel!, the speaker said he had not been in the town a very long time and it was very creditable indeed to them for not having said "This is a man not born in Warrington ; therefore we will let him go by" hut "This is a man of our trade and we will see him through." (Hear, hear and applause.) With one excep- tion Mr. Ansell had been a wiredrawer all him life, and that exception was when he served her Majesty in one of the fini st corps in the Army—lie R -iyal Horse Artillery, and during that time he had served his two years in the hot climate of India. Mr. Pierpoint said there had been expended in organisation a sum which he thought they would agree with him was very small for expenses and that was four guineas; Certain moness had been paid to Mr. Ansell during his siekness and that left that day a nett sum of £9B 143. 3d. (Loud ap- plause.) Mr. R. Davies had given half a sovereign when the otject was mentioned incidentally, and Mr. Carson had intimated that be would make the sum up to £lOO. Altogether there was a gross sum of £lO5 Bs. 3d. and a nett sum of £lOl 4a, 31. as the actual proceeds of the subscription or "draw." (applause.) OF that sum Mr. Ansell had already received a certain amount, and that day there would be handed over to him £93 18s. 31 The speaker added that as Mr. Ansell had not the use of his arms he hadlprevailed upon Mr.P. Birchall to receive the money on his behalf and requested him to may a few words in reply to the gift. They all hoped that Mr. Ansell would succeed in the work he was going to take in his native town of Birmingham. They wished him, his wile and children "God speed and good luck" Mr. Pierpoint then presented to Mr. Bimini!' the amount of the gift he had mentioned. Mr. BIRCHALL in replying, said he felt himself placed in a semewhat awkward, though pleasant position and that was to express the thanks of a man whom he had known for a few years and who had always conducted i himself in a creditable way. One characteristic of Edward Ansell was that talking was not his strong point. The speaker thanked Mr. Pierpoint for his able address and the wiredrawers of Warrington and all who had helped towards the subscription for that noble pur- pose. He felt honoured in taking part in the meeting that night because it was expressing the significant fact that there Was more practical benevolence getting abroad in the world. He did not think that sort of thing would have taken place 30 years ago and there was more practical Christianity even though it might be outside the churches perhaps. (Hear, hear.) It seemed to him that cases like that and the men who performed them were akin to the good Samaritan ' • it was the same spirit which actuated the men who had got op that presentation to help the brother who had " fallen by the way." Such practical sympathy was very pleas- ant and the speaker never telt more proud of the wire- drawers of Warrington than he felt when he knew what was going on in the matter. (Applause ) Mr. Pierpoint had done a kindly thing in coming done from London on that occasion. (Applause.) He thanked the Chairman and them all on behalf of Edward Ansell for the gift they had given him. Mr. W. Iladm proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Pier- point and Mr. J. CROWTHER seconded, the motion being carried unanimously.—ln reply Mr. PIERPOINT thanked the singers and accompanists for their help. He also tendered thanks to Mr. Pearson for supplying the piano and to Mr. Andrews of Padgate for the loan of the beautiful plants with which the platform was adorned. The proceedings then ,erminated.

ATHLETICS.

CYCLING.

So far the cycling season has been all that could be desired and as a cons,qm-nce the Saturday turnouts have been large. On Saturday last the home wheelers travelled to Siddington for the inter-club run with the Manchester Wheelers C.C. On j-iining together there was an imposing show for the Manchestrians "put in the field "more than as many as their friends. Glorious weather obtained and on reaching Siddington, a football match which had been looked forward to with pleasant feelings, was indulged in, the Warrington team being captained by Barber, the Warrington threequarter. As might be expected the Warringtonians had the best of it and eventually came off victors by 4 tries to nil, the scorers being Barber 2, Scott and Maguire one each. After partaking of an excellent tea the combined clubs went for a stroll round the mere, following which all proceeded to Knutstord where a smoking concert was held at the " Red Gew." Mr. Grime, uf the Wheelers, presided, and the excellent programme submitted was evidently appreciated by the cyclists. A start for home was made about tea o'clock, and the two clubs parted company at Mere amidst cheering and sounding of bells, whistles, &c: On Tuesday night the opening evening run took place, the destination being Hollins Green. Magnificent weather prevailed, and a large number of members took part in the run. •

CRICKET.

It is only a fortnight since the botball season closed. and yet cricket is in full swing, and it is astonishing to witness the large amount of clubs of greater or lesser importance, which havesprung up in the district. There are far more junior cricketers than footballers, and on every bit of waste ground about the town, the youthful Graces and Shrewsburys are to be seen.

The Warrington Cricket Club have made a wretched start in their fixtures, for they have already had two crushing defeats at the bands of Sefton and Preston re- spectively,and the latter, though not quite so decisive as the former,is the worst of the two because the thrashin4 was done at the Arpley enclosure. This is very dis- couraging, and does not give much hope of a victory over the powerful Manchester club. Warrington, how- ever, generally rises to the occasion when meeting Man- chester,and perhaps they will do so to-day. At any rate let us hope so.

--- The victory of Preston was largely due tc Jimmy 'toss, the well known association football player, who has been with the Liverpool club during the recent foot- ball season, but is better known in connection with Preston North End. Although he is a professional foot- baller, he is an amateur cricketer, and it is not impro- bable that he will be seen with the Lancashire county team during the present season. At the match on Saturday, he commenced very shakily, but he soon began to lay about him, and knocked up 42 in a very short time. With this exception, Preston were no better than Warrington. Only two Warrington men reached double figures, ' namely Bibby with 12, and Callon with 10 • while there were four "ducks." Roberts was chiefly responsible for the wholesale downfall of the Warnngton wickets, for he took seven wickets for only 18 runs, there being an average of less than a run per over taken off him. With him in such form it is hot surprising that Warring- ton only made 48. Of the five Warrington bowlers, Bates and Smith were the most successful. The skipper took three for 25, and the pro. floe for 25. Bates had been very unlucky in the first part of the innings and could not get a wicket. After a brief rest however, he resumed and then took five wickets for nine runs.

Warrington second eleven visited Go!borne, and more successful than their seniors, they made a draw of it, rather in favour of Goiborne. The last Golborne wicket was the most stubborn of the lot for no fewer than 26 were realised by it, otherwise Warrington would most probably have won. Tl,e visitors' fielding was every- thing that could be desired, and Duckworth and Cromp- ton were very successful with the ball.

The Sankey Weslevans always enjoy their fixture with the Windsor Wesleyans,for they are always certain of having a good time of it in every way. They were rather surprised last year when they were beaten, and they had the satisfaction this year of turning the tables upon their hosts. The Windsor Wesleyans have engaged the Liverpool Old Buys ground, and a capital cricket ground it makes. At the match on Saturday, Sankey Bridges batted first, and considering the bad start they made they were very fortunate in making 62, the chief contributors being Peers 20, and Beaman and Booth, 11 each. The match seemed a certainty for the home team when they had made 50 For the wickets, but strange to say, the last five fell for never a run. Beaman and Watkins were in fine form with the ball, the former taking five wickets for 16, and the latter five for 15.

After the match, the teams adjourned tothe Aigburth- street mission where the Win dsor people performed the duty of hosts with very great success, and a most enjoy- able musical evening was afterwards held. This match is looked forward to every year by both teams, and the return will be equally welcome. The Sankey Wire Mills with a weak team were by far the better of Belsby although operating away from home, and got the victory by atm lst two to one. Wood- cock was in rare form, and going in after the fall of the first wicket he proved superior to all comers,and carried his bat out for 50. This was not gained by fluky play, but by excellent cricket, and he actually scored twice as many as all the rest of the mill men put together. Mar- shall was the Most destructive bowler, and tie took five wickets, Evans coming next with three.

The match between the einploye of Peter Walker and Sens at Warrington and Ch-s'er was chiefly remarkable tor the quantity of ducks." In fact there were no fewer than eight of this species of fowl on the Chester side alone, and there must have been more time spent in walking to and from the wickets than in the innings itself. The Chester men would do well to practise alittle batting before the return match. A like remark would also apply to Moore and St. Ann's, for at a match between these two on Saturday,there were ten who could not raise a run between them.

It would be a good thing, seeing that there are so many clubs in the town, if some kind of competition were arranged, similar to the junior football teams. It would cause a great amount of intorest, and would tend to cause a feeling of healthy rivalry between the various clubs, as well as bringing out to more conspicuous notice sonic tif the promising young cricketers. There was some talk about this matter a year or two ago, but the thing did not "catch on," arid it is rather unfortunate that there does not seem to Ll•-?, any better prospect at the present time.

FOOTISA LL.

Football has now to take back seat, but there are still many matters of inter...lt going on in connection with it. It was at one Om, feared that there was going to be serious trouble betw. 1-II the clubs in the senoir competition and the Lanca.iitre County Committee, but at a meeting on Tuesday ek..•ning, the matter was ami- cably settled, and the corn I,etition will be proceeded with after all. As is well kn x•i,there will be an increase of two clubs in the league ri •ict year,and as the football season is reduced in length (~. one muith,the competition will be of a much Moro exac-ing nature than previously.

Urijah Thomas. the chairman of the Congregational Union rose on Tuesday evening in the 'City Temple to deliver his address, there were few va- rant seats in the large building, and before he had ' finished there was not standing room. Mr. Thomas managed to make himself well heard nearly the whole of the time he was speaking, and he was frequently inter- ' rupted by vigorous applause. His subject was " Brotherhood," the cry for which, he said, was one of the clearest of the age. A volowing appeal to his hearers to cultivate the " gathering sense of brotherliness" closed an elevating and inspiriting address. Nest in ' importance only to the address of the chairman of the Union was the presentation of the testimonial to Dr. ' Guinness Rogers at the Memorial Hall on Tuesday after- noon. Succeeding speakers earnestly piled on compliments, till Dr. Rogers seemed quite overwhelmed when his turn came to acknowledge them. While this interesting ceremony was taking place, the ladies of the London Missionary Society were holding an immenFely successful 'meeting, in Westmins- ter Chapel, with Mr.flugh Matheson in the chair.

"IS WAR A NECESSARY EVIL ?"

ADDRESS BY A LADY MINIS FEE.

On Sunday evening at the Friends' Meeting House, Buttarmarket-street, Miss Ellen Robinson, a minister of the Society of Friends, gave an address on the subject "Is war a nece,sary evil ? " She said when we called upon men to give up the heathen and barbarous practices of war we were preaching the gospel of Jesus Christ and endeavnuring to apply the speaking and teaching of the Lead to the ethics of our life here on earth. If the Christian Ckurch had even followed the example uf the early Christians we should not he where we are now in the present position of the worl,l. There should not be great nations calling themselves Christian and claiming to be followers of the Lord, and tee,' acting as heathens and barbarians in then- c..nduct towards one another. The speaker said that neatdv every Christian Church in the land had at one time or another passed a resolutioteat their meetings as congregational bodies, in favour of arbitration as a means of settling disputes between nation,,, instead or war. They were glad that there was such a growing recognition—more than most people had any idea of— if the terrible anomaly of war in a Christian land. War was a living lie in a Christian land, and they deeply lamented that there was else a growth and activity, &pressing forward of great military proposals and an advance on the question of militarism as well :as on the aide of peace. They wriuld heed that in our own country whilst there had been this growth in the peace question there had also been, in late years, great growth in the cause of militarism and we never before spent in peace time so much on our armaments. Never had such enormous sums been laid out in prepar- ation for war: hewer were there so many armed men on the continent of Eurepe as at the present time ; there were millions and millions of meu trained to the 1393 of arms and rips to destroy one anbther sheuld war break out in Europe. Alluding to the military training of some Sunday school children, Miss Robinson remarked what a terrible confusion it was between the ideas of spiritual warfare And the outside warfare which was the exact opposite of the spiritual, and one wondered how the children could learn to distinguish at all between such wide apart teachings. Ti) her mind this was one of the saddest features Of the military movement—this training of children before they were old enough to think for themselves. She had seen these military or- ganisations at their annual meetings, and heard many things said which were quite right, and then she had seen the lads brought in in semi-militaly dress with weapons and going through all sorts of military move- ments This was done under the sanction of the Sunday school, and the same lips that taught them to love their enemies taught them that it was right for them to kill, and tester and destroy. ()Their food for reflection was the great number of wars that were being continually car- ried on against the weaker races of the earth. We were not just towards the weak, and there was no standard of international justice, and because we were not just we were afraid to be weak. The speaker wished tier audience to -realise that it was not an abstract question but a burning and pressing mestion of this day and hour. Referring to the query "Is were necessary evil," Miss Robinson declared it was not, and she produced many quotations and arguments in support of her statement. War was an evil not only in the sense of suffering but in the moral wrong doing. What was to prevent them ektahlishinz a es-stem of justice between nations such as existed betwe( n ',- valuate in our own country. At the end f the 19 ti Christian century we knew of no better way ha sett mat; disputes than to come to blows. le was a she ine on the world, on humanity-,nn ciyilization,and on the Christian- ity of the world that arbitration did not obta.!n before this. Wars were idiotic ; they did not settle disputes ex- cept for • a moment, and then the old flames broke out again And again. Toe speaker earnestly desired that all,as individualtamight feel their responsibility in the matter. Throughout the address, which Miss Robinson delivered with great impressiveness and faith, the audience mani- fested the deepest interest, and time words of the speaker I had in many cases evidently "fallen upon good ground."

THE reception given. to Her The Liberal Majesty's ministers at the Party. National Liberal Club on Wed- nesday evening appears to have been a very successful affair. There have been so many rumours of disunion in the Liberal party and of disagreements amongst ministers themselves that the gathering answered the double purpose of an exercise of genial hospitality by the members of the Club and of a convincing refutation of the baseless inventions of interested 'politicians. It was not an occasion for set speeches but for all that there was speaking of an interesting charac- ter. The PRIME MINISTER has not yet by any means, it seems, recovered his former physical vigour, yet his address on this occasion shows no signs of disencouragernent or of that impending break-up of the party which he leads, which has been so often prophesied. Last year at a similar' gathering he declared that he was prepared to carry on the government of the country with a majority of two if necessary. This year he prac- tically reiterates that declaration, and, what is more, he is evidently of the opinion that during the last twelve reonths the Liberal party has really gained rather than lost strength in all essen- tial particulars. He contends that it is a failing of the party to look too much at its own difficult ties while shutting its eyes to the difficulties of those opposed to it. As a matter of fact minis- ters have done well in the House of Commons. Mr. Metall' " pulverised "' Mr. CHAMBERLAIN in his speech on the Address; Mr. FOWLER by a splen- did and convincing speech completely thwarted an intrigue for the defeat of the Government on the Indian .cotton duties; Sir Edward GREY, as Under Secretary for Foreign Affairs, has fairly won, in distinction at all events,the epaulettes of a Cabinet Minister, while last but not least there has been the indefatigable and brilliant leader- ship of Sir William HARCOURT. Oa the other hand, looking at the front Opposition bench what was to he seen ? Certainly not a" union of hearts. The incidents in connection with the Speakership and the Leamington and other elections has shown quite clearly that the bond which holds the two sections of the Opposition is one of pure self-interest and of extreme frailty. For the time being the differ- ences between Mr. CHAMBERLAIN and his small band of followers and the Tapers and Tadpoles of the Tory party have been smoothed over, brit no- body expects that the truce will be of long dura- tion. The necessities of opposition and the eager ' desire to secure a majority at tho next election keep the" Unionists " in some sort of order, but once let that majority be gained it is almost in- evitable that, in the greedy scramble for office, the two sections will again be at loggerheads. On the whole, therefore: LORD ROSEBERY is amply justified in taking a favourable view of the future of the Liberal party as compared with that of the Unionists.

The visit of this selected team to the Antipodes seems to have been in every respect an unqualified success. All of the players speak in.the warmest terms of the hospitality of the Colonials. Austra- lians, it would appear, are born cricketers, and nothing has struck the players from England so much as the number of "young hangs" that were fit to be placed in the field even in first-class matches. Australian spectators, too, are models of magnanimity. They applauded with great enthusiasm when their own men were scoring freely, but their applause was just as loud when STODDART or MACLAREN, BROWN or WARD were piling up their magnificent scores, or when the bowling of PEEL or RICIIARDSON was proving des- tructive. From a financial point of view the suc- cess of the tour has been remarkable. At the fins,: test match over four thousand pouil at the gate, and as the receipts on other occasions were in proportion it is not surprising to learn that the bodies who prcmoted the tour have made some thousands of pounds profit. It is said that steps are already being taken to arrange for a visit to England of an Australian team in 1896, and

THE Budget which Sir WILLIAg The Budget ri ........ARCOURT submitted to the House of Commons last week contained nothing of a sensational or even of a controversial character. There was no proposal to impose ad- ditional taxation, nor, with the exception of the spirit duties, to remit any tax which had been previously paid. On the whole the statement of the CHANCELLOR of the EXCHEQER was a satisfac- , tory one, though it would doubtless disapPoint I those who have been expecting some stroke ol finance in the direction of a "free breakfast table?
