RAND OF HOPE PICNIC. On Saturday afternoon the annual treat given to the members of the St. John's Presbyterian Band of Elope society took place. About 150 children and friends journeyed by train to Frodsham. An enjoyable time was spent on Overton Hills in games, racing, /cc., and during their sojourn on the hills tea was part .ken of. Gums' FRIENDLY SOCIETY, RUNCORN. The Rev. Canon Maitland Wood conaucted an ad- mission service in St. Michael's Church, on Thursday evening in connection with the Runcorn branch of the above-mentioned society. The branch also embraces Weston Halton, and Aston, but the twenty candidates admitted all belonged to Runcorn. NARROW ESCAPE FRO/d DROWNING AT Rtuccoorr. A boy named William Farridy 3, Peel-st., had a narrow escape from drowning in the Bridgewater Canal on Sunday afternoon,between four and live o'clock. It appears he was playing with a small boat which he had placed upon the water, and in endeavouring to reach it he overbalanced himself and fell into the canal. The Incident was witnessed by two men, who were close at hand, and they immediately rescued the little fellow. He was taken home not much the worse for his immer- sion.

CHESHIRE COUNTY COUNCIL SCHOIARSHIPS. At Greenway-road Board schools on Wednesday, an examination of scholars from elementary schools in Run- corn district for the twenty scholarship 4 offered by the County Council was held. There were 23 candidates ; seven boys and five girls from the Board schools ; six from Brunswick Wesleyan schools -, three from the National ; one from Hely Trinity, and one from Halton. The candidates were examined by a superintendent appointed by the City Council. The successful candi- dates will be entitled to free tuition at one of the schools specified by the Council, and books and travell- ing expenses will be paid for. THE •TECHNICAL INSTITUTR. . . .

A meeting of the sub-committee of the Technical In- struction Committee was held on Tuesday. Mr.Wiswa;l presided. The question as to, who should open the In- stitute was brought forward, and it was resolved tat Mr. Brunner be written to asking if he could undertak, the duty about the middle of June. The subjects to be taught at the Institute were under consideration. It was found that the grant from the County Council would permit of the following subjects being taught, and these were decided upon :—Freehand and model, geometry, building construction machine construction, physi- ography, applied mechanics, steam, French, shorthand, dress making, carpentry, and j)inery, alkali manufac- ture, soap manufacture, electricity and magnetism, sound, light, and heat, plumbing, astronomy, naviga- tion and shipbuilding, mathematics, theoretical mechan- ics, theoretical chemistry, elementary and advanced, iractical chemir cookery, and laundry work.

At the Runcorn Police Court on Thursday morning, William White, late of Runcorn, was brought up undPr a warrant charged with neglecting to maintain his wife who had become chargeable to the Union—Mr. Cooke, relieving officer, Runcorn, stated teat the prisoner's wife had received an order or Si. per week made upon the defendant wlso afterwards became charg,able to the Union. The prisoner went to Fleetwood where he found employment at the Chemical works. Be was brought from Fleetwood the previous day. The relief given amounted to Ms.—The prisoner promised to pay a por- tion of the money, and the other portion of the amount would be advanced, in all £1 19. 81., and on doing thi 3 the case was withdrawn.—At the same court Eleanor Wakefield was fined Si. and costs for being drunk and disorderly in High-street. THE CHAPLAIN OF THE MERSEY MISSION TO SEAMEN..

-- The committee of the Mersey Mission to Seamen, with tin approval of the Bishaps of Liverpool and Chester, have appointed the Rev. Edgar Lambert, M. A., of Pem- broke College. Cambridge, senior chaplin of the mission to succeed the Ven. Archdeacon Woosnam, who has recently accepted the living of St. Margaret's, Dunham Massey, Altrincham. Mr. Lambert is well-known to sailors throughout the world by his successful work for nine years as chaplain of the missions to seamen in Sun- derland, a post he vacated three years ago when he was appointed to the living of Heene, Worthing, by the Archbishop of Canterbury. This living he at present holds. Mr. Lambert is also well-known to university men, since he was president of the Cambridge boat in the year 1882. He is an ardent lover of sailers, and has shown himself an efficient organiser in work amongst them.

TEI-E-.REV. T. BRACHRNEDRY AND CHRISTIAN .UNITY.

The Rev. T. Brackenbury preaching in St. Paul's %Yes- levan Church,on Sunday evening, made reference to the proposed re-uni9n of the churches. He based his &s- -cours.. on the words, " What is the church ?" Matthew 16 c., 18 v. After referring to the many senses in which the word "church" was used, he proceeded to show what was the true church. He observed that Lord Halifax, the President of the English Church Union, had openly advocated the re-union of the English and Rom- ish Churches. He had recently been to Rome, and had returned elated with hope that this would be accom- plished. The Pope bad also sent a letter, not to the Roman Catholic churches, but to the people of England, in which he entreated them to return to the Roman Church. He and his people were praying to the saints and the Virgin Mary for their conversion. But they would rather not. Let Popery renounce its deadly errors and then they would have no objection to he united to it. They had already a true evangelical unity, not the unity of that of a policeman and a prisoner handcuffed together, but a unity like that of the ocean, the billows were distinct but the sea wAs one. The unity they had was the fusion of hearts, a oneness of affection • they were members If Christ, and therefore members o each otber.

At the County Magistrates' Court, Liverpool, on Wednesday, before Ildessra. G. W.Goodison and J. Mack, John James, captain of the Blanche Riok, was sum- moned by the Board of Trade for . overloading that vessel at Runcorn, on the 28th March. Mr. Paxton, who appeared for the Board of Trade, said the vessel was usually engaged in the coasting trade, but in the present case she had taken on 13,)ard a cargo of salt for Leland. The vessel had left the dock and entered the tidal basin when she was observed by the Board's stir- veyer (Captain Jeffrey), who noticed that she was over- laden to the depth of four inches. The disc was below water. The attention of the master and managing owner was directed to the state of the steamer, when thirty-three tons of salt were drawn out of the vessel. She was then brought up to the mark, and allowed to go to sea. The prosecution was laid under section 442 el the Merchant Shipping Act.—Mr. Pedder submitted that the defendant was not responsible f,r the overloading. The regular master of the steamer held a coasting.certi- flcate.and in was necessary to engage Captain James for this voyage. There was no intention on the part of the owners or the defendant to overload the vessel, and as I soon as it was known that the vessel was overloaded the mistake was rectified.—The Bench, who did not think the owners would have allowed the vessel to go to sea, inflicted a small penalty of £5 and costs. POLICE CAsEs. _ _ .

On Wednesday morning- at Runcorn Police Court, I Felix McConn, was brought before !dr. T. Rigby (chair- man) and Mr. F. J. Norman. charged with being drunk and disorderly in Water-street, on the 17th July, 1894. The prisoner, who appeared with his shoulder dislocated, had been apprehended under a warrant fir tailing to appear in answer to a summons on the 23rd July. He now pleaded guilty.—P.C. Oregery statei that on the date named the prisoner was drunk and was fighting with his wife. They were both summoned, hut the prisoner did not appear. His wife did so and she was sent to gaol for fourteen days.—Superintendent Atherton said that the prisoner when in drink was a violent character. Br some means or other he had got his shoulder out,and he did not wish to press the case against him, lie had promised to be teetotal and to turn over a new leaf. In answer to the magistrates as to how he get his shoulder put out he said he did not know.—Superin- tendent Atherton said the prolasbility was that the pri- soner was stupidly drunk and had fallen. Ho had been locked up since Monday night and had been attended tc; by a doctor. On promising to sign the pledge he was discharged.—Three persons were apprehinded under a warrant for not appearing: at the Court on the previous Monday to answer a summons for neglecting to send their children regularly to school. The first prisoner dealt with was John liazley, Mill Brow, who stated that he was work- ing on Monday. and thought his wife would have at- tended. the Court to answer the summons.---Superinten- dent Atherton said the warrants were issued because People were not observing the order of the Justicpg. They seemed to make game of the whole thing from beginning to end. The sty had been taken to disabuse the mind of the public of the idea that they could do what they liked when they received a summons. -The Chairman told the prisoner that nest time he received a I summons lie must pay more attention to it or there would be more serious consequences. He would he fined 61. and 4s. 61. costs.—Joseph Basnett, High-st., and Joseph Wakefield. Loch-st., were similarly dealt I with for a like offence.

THE EXAMINER, SATUROAY, MAY 11 1895

I • THE VACANCY IN NEWTOWN WARD. No steps have yet been taken to fill the vacancy in Newtown ward caused by the failure of Mr. W. M. Williams. The Council will most probably consider the matter when the official statement as to the bankruptcy of the member in question has been received. Several names have alreauy been mentioned as suitab:e candi- dates. FORTHCOMING BILLIARD MATCH. As will be seen from an advertise:nent in another column a grand billiard match will be played at the Runcorn Liberal Club on Wednesday evening. The contestants will be the well-known cueists C. Dawson and E. Diggle.

their food supplies, but too much money was spent on she army. Touching upon the Home Rule question, the tpeaker said that the Tory party, if that measure were not mentioned at a political meeting carried out that it had been dropped. It was therefore incumbent upon him to give the present measure his full approval. Though it had been impossible to legislate for Ireland for the past three years, it had not been impossible to administer the law in a satisfactory manner, and that was a matter upon which the Government could con- gratulate themselves. There had been a diminution of crime through the land. Referring to the House of Lords, he said it was impossible for the Liberal party to go on with that body as at present constituted. They must never forget Mr. Gladstone' last speech in the House of Commons,in which he told them that the posi- tion held now by the House of Lords was intolerable to the Liberal party. Mr. Deacon concluded by referring to the Loral Veto Bill, which he said was an improve- ment on that introduced last session. (Applause.) Councillor O'Donnell proposed,and Mr. Horan seconded a resolution calling upon the Government to investigate the cases of Irish pAlitical prisoners. This was passed, and the meeting concluded with the usual votes of thanks.

MARRIAGE OF lilt. A. F. MANN.

On Thursday, the marriage was solemnised at Christ Church, Mayfair, London, of Mr. Albert Fred Mann, clerk to the Prescot Board of Guardians. , second son of Mr. Edward Mann, of Duumow, Essex, to Miss Mary Wood, eldest daughter of Mr. Thomas Wood, of Umbledon, Gloucester. The bride was given away by her step-brother. She wore a dress of rich pale blue satin and a bonnet of gold passementerie, trimmed with white velvet ribbon and orange blossom. She carried a bouquet of choice white flowers, and wore a gold bracelet, the gift of the bridegroom. The bridesmams, Miss Kate Mann and Miss Emily Claydyn, were attired in cornflower blue crepon, trimmed with lace and ribbon, and hate of black chip, trimmed with corn- flower, ribbon, and roses. They were presented by the bridegroom with gold bracelets and bouquets of pink roses. The best man was Mr. W. J. Glover, of St. Helens, and the Rev. H. Russell, vicar of Christ Church, was the officiating clergyman. The wed- ding party were entertained by Mr. and Mrs, Rus- sell at 12e, Curzon-st., Mayfair. The bride's travelling dress was of electric blue cloth with white vest, and hat of sun-burnt straw trimmed with rich electric blue velvet. The presents were numerous and costly, and included a case of silver and a beautifully-iiluminated address from the board of guardians, and a silver tea and coffee ser- vice and address from the officials of the union. After a short stay at 'forging, Mr. and Mrs. Mann will take up their residence at Eccleston Park.

Baguio? presided. An interesting feature of the concert was the magic lantern illustrations to the various items given during the evening. These included "The Village Blacksmith," "Death of Nelson," " John Gilpin" and " Allan Water." A number of views of North Wales and of English places of interest were also exhibited.' Mr. Robinson of Runcorn, was the lanternist. A quar- tette party consisting of Miss Jones, Miss I3irkett, Mr. Morris and Mr. Birkett gave " The Song of the River" and "Allan Water," and duets entitled " The Pilot Brave "and " When the wind bloweth in from the sea" were respectively given by Miss Looker and Mr. P. Brad- bury; and Miss Jones and Mr. Morris. The Rev. J. H. rite sang a sacred solo "The Good Shepherd " and Miss Lizzie Ryalls recited "John Gilpin." Miss Clara Taylor, Mr. T. Barrow., Mr. Morris, and Mr. H. Rowe contri- buted solos and they met with hearty receptions. Mr. John Birkatt gave a humorous song entitled " The tin Gee-gee." The duties of accompanist were satisfactorily fulfilled by Mr. William Cooke. At the conclusion of what proved a most successful concert a vote of thanks was passed to all those who had assisted on the proposi- tion of the Rev. J. H. Tite. BOWLING.—HALTON v. 147 75 HAYDOCK.

. THE URBAN DIS URICT COUNCIL. The usual monthly meeting of this Council was held last week, this being the first meeting of the Council since the day of meeting was changed. Mr. J. Pilling presided, and there was present a fair attendance of naembers.—Dr. Hayward, in his quarterly report, stated that the estimated population of the township, as deter- mined by the usual calculation, was 6.843, but there were good reasons for believing that the actual popula- tion exceeded 7.000. During the quarter there were registered : births-42 males, 46 lemales,total 88; deaths —52 males, 22 females, total 54. rhe birth rate was 51.4 per thousand per annum, death rate 228, zymatic rate 14•0, infant mortality under one year old 215 per , thousand births. The birth rate was very unusually high and the death rates were unfortunately very high. The epidemic ot measles might be said to have raised the death rate to the extent of 16 per thousand per annum. Scarlet fever had broken out, the first case being notified on February Bth. In order to prevent the spread among children he Wrote to the schoolmasters and mistresses suggesting that it any of the teachers became aware of any children having a very slight sore throat or present- ing any suspicious appearance of rough scaling skin they should be at once sent home. During the first two months of the quarter influenza had been again exten- sively prevalent as an epidemic. Only owe &lath had , occurred from this disease.—Dr. Dowling asked the Medical Officer if it was a fact that a case of small-pox had occuri ed.—Dr. Hayward replied that it was so. The man was now in the Old Wint Hospital. The man had been to several townships hi the district, and when the disease was discovered he was in St. Helens. With reference to the insanitary condition of the privies in the neighbourhood of Toll-bar, which was complained of at a previous meeting by Dr. Dowling,Dr. Hayward reported that he had inspected the property known as Toll-bar Cottages, were, since the beginning of the year, there had been notified twelve cases of infectious disease, out of a total of 28 cases In the whole township, excluding the eases of scarlet fever, which were probably not much related to insanitary conditions. The chief defects discovered r) him was the faulty construction of the privies an,l ash s. rhey were all uncovered and ton deep, se that the contents, in many cases, were liquefied. The worst «samples were found in the two rows of houses on the north side of Judkeld-st., but the work of filling them up to the extent of 18 Inches iii efficiently draining them was in progress. In the other rows of houses where the ash pits were less deep, the work was also begun of draining them. When this work was carried out a considerable improvement would be effected. The medical officer condemned the systPm of privy construc- tion in existence in the township, and submitted a piak of the standard required in St. Helens, which he con- sidered should be adopted at least in the construction of new houses. The plan of draining ashpits into sewers was not to be recommended, as it tended to choke up the sewers. It was desirable that in a very large pro- portion of cases the privies should be re-constructed,hut in the meantime, unless the Council were prepared to compel re-construction in a wholesale manner, they would have to make the best of the existing arrange- ments and effect improvements by degrees.—Dr. Dow- !ing protested strongly against the present system of ash ' pits. He thought they ought to be re constructed up to model requirements, but he would not make any pro- position, remarking that he had done his best in the matter.

MURCHISON GIFT GOLD Miantso Co—" Truth " May 9, says :—Considerable dealings are reported in the shares of the Murchison Gift Gold Co. at 54. to 53. 61., and the outlook bids fair for a good rise. This Company has acquired the Christmas 13:or property near the well- known mines. A fair amount of development work has been carried out, and the reports state that in two shafts sunk the reef has been proved. The capital of the Company is £60,000 of 103. shares, 83. paid.' AFRICAN COAL AND EXPLORATION —A short time Since we directed attention to the shares of this Company when the price WAS only about 53. 6.1. while it after- wards rose to 10s. 61. Thus those woo bought at the time had the opportunity of nearly douoling their money. A slight reaction subsequently took place bringing the price down to 84. 3d. to 81. 91. the present quotation at which figure our information goes they should be bought once more for a greater rise than ever. The market is in the hands of influential parties who will not rest with quotation at the present low level. A " little bird " whispers what great developments have taken place on the property. This if true,will work wonders in regard to enhancing the value of the shares. This little bird has a far reachin?, eye and has consequently a happy knack of being able to whisper of events far bey•rino the range of vision of ordinary mortals:. It must therefore suffice to say that the shares should assuredly be bought, once more.

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT

RUNCORN.

BY OUR RUNCORN CORRESPONDENT.

It is pleasing to learn that preparations are being made for the commencement of the work in respect of the agreement which has been entered into by the Runcorn Council and the Manchester Ship Canal Co., for the provision of extra accommodation and other facilities at Runcorn. Already some of the material for the slipways has been landed at Runcorn, and it is not improbable that the labour required will be let out by contract. With regard to the work proposed to be done at the Old Quay, this, it is uaderstood, will be placed in the hands of Mr. Wiswall, C.E. manager of the Sprinch works at Runcorn, belonging to the Canal Co. He will also have in hand the erecting of the bridge over the canal at Halton-road. Plans are now being prepared. and there is every likelihood of the work being well in hand in the course of a short space of time.

The Runcorn Ferry has now become a bone of contention between the Runcorn and Widnes Councils on the one hand, and the Ship Canal Com- pany on the other. It seems that the -Company have imposed a toll of one penny for each passenger crossing the waterway which is in addition to that already charged for ferrying the river. Another matter in which dissatisfaction is expressed is the charge made by the Company to boatmen when they cross the canal with their boats for the pur- pose of placing them on the beach. This is mani- festly unfair when it is considered that the canal company intercepted the strand on the Cheshire side of the River at Runcorn in the construction of the canal. Not much use has been made of the ferry since it was re-started, and unless the Canal Company re-mit their charge it is not at all likely that the traffic will increase. The question arises whether the Canal Company are legally entitled to impose a fare. This is one of the points which will no doubt be tested by the authorities.

When persons fail to answer a police summons they run a risk of being unceremoniously locked up if the magistrates issued a warrant But such a drastic step is seldom taken in regard to school attendance cases, and consequently a number of parents when summoned have treated the matter with contempt, and stayed away from the court altogether. On Monday out of a batch of persons summoned for neglecting to send their children regularly to school four did not put in an appear- ance and the magistrates decided to set their backs up against this defiance of the Court and ordered warrants to be issued. Three of the recalcitrant de- fendants were captured on Wednesday morning and brought to the Police Station. Their excu,e to the magistrates was that the matter had been left in the hands of their wives who had neglected to attend the Police Court. When the men are at work they cannot be expected to leave it for the purpose of appearing at the Police Court to answer a school attendance summons, when their wives can do just as well as themselves. But their duty lies in see- ing that the matter is attended to, and no doubt the action of the magistrates will have a beneficial effect in this direction.

The report of Dr. Adams, the medical officer for the Runcorn Rural district on the sanitary condi- tion of the 36 townships in his district, is a most comprehensive one, and although some progress in sanitary reform is reported there yet remains much to be accomplished. No fewer than 202 notifica- tions of infectious disease were received during the year, which, with the exception of 1893 when smallpox was so prevalent, exceeds all previous years. The source of infection in a number of cases is attributed to an unsatisfactory water supply,and this is one of the difficulties often met with in coun- try districts, as the drinking water is taken from wells and public purnp,i,and is far from being R 8 pure as it should be. Another serious cause mentioned is the insanitary condition of houses ; and considering the powers local authorities now possess for dealing with such property, this source of infection should be more easily rectified. For in- stance Dr. Adams reports as follows regarding some property at Halton where two fatal cases of typhoid fever occurred. There existed, he save " A serious combination of insanitary conditions, on which I have previously reported ; the houSes have no thorough ventilation, the roofing and spouting were defective, a deep gully runs behind the houses, and at times being filled with sewage from cowsheds and piggeries, which, with the sewer blocked, caused the sewerage to filter through the basement into the houses, and might actually be seen oozing into the inner surfaces of the houses" With such a condition of things can it be wondered at that disease should PRi ?

===== 0002601_18950511_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
WIDNES DIV1;-'ION LIBERAL ASSOCIATION.

ADDRESS BY MR. H. WADE DEACON.

On Monday evening a meeting of the electors of the polling districts of Wide's was hell in the Alford-street Chambers. The business transacted was the election of a committee consisting of 208 to serve on the Rime- sentative Council. and the selection of 26 persons to act as an Executive Committee. Mr. E.Carey presided, and among those present were—Messrs, H. Wade Deacon, J.P., C.C.' Stuart Deacon, J. W. Warning, S. Sadler, F. Hartland. J. Cosier, D. Lewis, Dr. O'Keeffe, W. Ware- ing, A. G. Kyle. T. Sherratr, H. O'Donnell, P. Horan, J. Sadler, J. Griffiths, C. Bridgstock, J. Prescott, U. B. Shores (secretary), and J. Duckworth. TIIE LIVERPOOL RAILWAY MISSION.

The annual tea in connection with the above mission was held in St. Peter's schoolrornn, John-st.,on Saturday evening. An excellent tea was provided by Miss S. Green, the missioner for the railway. The tables were presided over by Mesdames Percival. Rogers, Critchley, Bate. Pearson, and the Misses Percival, Green, James, and Hindley. A public meeting was afterwards held, presided over by the Rev. B. Percival, vicar of St. Peter's, and there were also present the Revs.J. A. Cooil, P. J. Byrne, Miss Beetle Dutton (Moore), Miss Green,and Mr. Thompson,of Liverpool. Mr. Thompson read letters of apology from Sir E. R. Russell and the Mayor of Warrington, and Mr. E. J. Guest, J.P.

_ . The Cnemarms expressed his appreciation of the good work which was being done by Miss Green in connection with the mission, and hoped that her labours would be abundantly blessed. In her annual report, Miss Green said that during the year, she had much cause tor thankfulness. All the stations had been visitsal,and !Alt bad received a hearty welcome when visiting the homes of the sick. A Prayer Union and Temperance League had recently been formed and 90 members had iiined the Prayer Union and 58 the Temperance League. ',Applause.) 'I lre number of meetings which had been conducted at different places during the year was as follows : At the Arpley Mission, , 52 ; Waggon Works, 51 ; London and North-Western ! Sheds, 36 ; Bank Quay Station, 22 ; Central Station, 22; Birkenhead Goods Department, London and North- Western, 34 ; Birkenhead Goods Department, Cheshire Lines Conmpttee, 34 ; Buckley-at., 1 ; Ragged School,4 ; Workingmen's Gospel Temperance Mission, 1 ; Temper- ance Meeting, Moore, 1 ; Gospel flail, 1 ; St. Barnanas' Band of Hope, 5 ; Workhouse, 4 ; making a grand total of 268. The number of tracts distriouted was 12,000; stations visited, 300 ; and the Infirmary 22 times.—The report was adopted, on the motion of Mr. Wildman, seconded by Mr. Massey. Miss DurroN, who was received with applause, said she knew Miss Green, and she should be pleased to say what she could to encourage her in the work in which , she was engaged. With regard to the Railway Mission, the men in it worked with might and main, and she was sure they did all they could to induce their companions to take an interest in the work and to get good, and , then they found that many a time the promises they obtained were not fulfilled, and that the people they had hoped to see at their meetings were not present. Addresses were also given by Messrs. Tnornpson, P. Knowles (C.L.C.), J. Barton (C.L.C.), and J. J. Harding and Mrs. Rogers. On Sunday, a number of railway men, headed by the Briek-street Brass Band,assernbled at Bank Quay Station at 1.50 p.m., after which they marched in procession along Sankey-street, Horsemarket-street, and Winwick- street to St. Ar.n's Church, where a special service was held. Mr. J. A. Thompson delivered an excellent address upon the words " For I am not ashamed of the gospel of Christ ; for it is the power of God unto salvation to everyone that believeth ; to the Jew first, and also to the Greek." A collection was made to defray the expenses of the mission. A special service was held in the evening in St. Ann's Cturch, at the conclusion of the ordinary evening service, for railway men and their wives, when Mr. Thompson delivered another suitable address. There were good congregations, afternoon and evening.

TRAP ACCIDENT.—A trap accident occurred at Frod- sham Bridge on Wednesday night. About nine o'clock a trap containing Mr. J. H. Davies and Mr 4, Thomas DAVIPS, of Five Crosses, Frodshatn, was nearing Frodsham Bridge when a wag- gonette coming in the itprosite direction driven by Thomas Plevin, ostler at the Bear's P 4 w Inn, FreAsham, collided with it. Mrs. Davies was precipitated out of the trap and fell heavily on the ground. breaking her shoulder bone. The driver of the waggonette was also thrown to the ground, hut he escaped with a shaking. The injured lady was conveyed home and Dr. Burton was sent for, and on examination found she had sus- tained serious injuries. It was quite light at the time of the accident. THURSDAY.

In the House of Commons on Thursday evening the debate on Cie motion of the third reading of the London and North-Western Railway Bill was resumed, and tbe action of the Company in regard to certain Welsh-speak- ing workmen was made the ground of further opposi- tion. Mr. F. EDWARDS moved that the bill be recom- mitted in respect of clauses 13 and 14, and Mr. J. L. MORGAN seconded the motion. In supplrting the motion Mr. Ltoro-Gsosnit said they objected to any further powers being given to the London and North-Western Railway Company in Wales. Mr. PLUNRRT said that if Welsh members took the responsibility of striking out these particular clauses he had no objection, VI order to facilitate the progress of the measure. The motion for recommittal was rejected by 233 to 106. As a further protest against the conduct of the Company, the Welsh members again divided the House on the motion for third reading. The third reading was carried by 243 to 120.

Mr. CAMPBELL-BANNERUAN, in reply to Mr. GROVE, said that the Committee on the Unemployed was engaged in collecting information, and he trusted that it would be able in a few weeks to come to its conclusions on the paints before it. Sir G. TREVELYAN moved the appointment of a Stand- ing Committee for the consideration of Government measures relating to Scotland, such committee to con- sist of all the members representing Scotch constitu- encies, together with fifteen other members, to be nominated by the Committee of Selection. Sir El. fileitwELL moved an amendment declining to entertain a proposal to refer Bills to Standing Committees on the basis of separate nationalities. The amendment was defeated by 186 to 162. The debate was adjourned.

===== 0002601_18950511_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
SHIP CANAL. THE NAVIGATION OF THE MERSEY. MESSRS. FAIRCLOUGII v. THE SHIP CANAL COMPANY.

A special Divisional Court, consisting of the Lord Chief Justice, Mr. Justice Charles, and Mr. Justice Wright,sat on Thursday to dispose of the point arising out of the litigation between Messrs. James Fairclough and Sons, millers, of Cooper's Wharf, Warrington, and the Man- ' chester Ship Canal Company. Mr. Jlsepti Walton, Q C., and Mr. Hughes were for the plaintiffs, and Mr. Cripps, Q.C., and Mr. C. A. Russell were for the defendants. The point for decision arise. out of the arbitration which has been suspended psnding the decision of the case stated. Mr. Joseph Walton,Q C. was about to reopen the facts, when the Lord Chief Justice remarked that his brothers had beard the matter before, and he had read the case. The question resolved itself entirely into a question of the true construction of section 82 of the Manchester Ship Canal Act. Mr. Walton proceeded to read the section which, it will be remembered, referred entirely to the protection of the entrances to dooks, &c., at Widnes. He conten- ded that the Canal Company seemed to think that so long as there was a channel, although it might have gone over to the other side of the river Mersey, the wharf-owners had no remedy under the section unless they could show it was assignable to the works of the company: .

Mr. Cripps said the wharf owners were outside the section. He thought there would be no remedy when it could be proved under the Lands Clauses Act that the silting up was due to the construction as apart from the maintenance,or that there had been some negligence on the part of the company. As regarded the local silting, it was not a part of the defendants' works. There was a constant silting going on in the Mersey, and if the owner took the trouble to dredge that away it would be all right, but here was a claim for £2,000 up to a date sonse months tack ; were the Company to dredge away to Runcorn and give the plaintiffs free ton- nage?

The Lord Chief Justice: An the owner wants is a fair way ot travelling. As long as the main channel was kept open it was not their business to deal with local sittings. The dredging the Company had to perform was such as would enable vessels to enter or leave the Canal.

Mr. Jogeph Walton replied on the whole case. Their Lordships reserved judgment, as it was a ques- tion of great importance, and there were other similar cases dependent on it. The Lord Chief Justice intimated that judgment.would.be delivered at an early date.

===== 0002601_18950511_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
SALFORD SEWAGE PIPE LITIGATION.

At the Chancery Court of Lancashire, held at the Assize Courts, Strar.geways, on Wednesday, Vice- Chancellor Robinson cave judgment in an action which had been brought by the Ship Canal Company against the Salford Corporation. It was a motion for an injunc- tion to restrain the Corporation from laving a pipe be- tween their sewage works at Mode Wheel and the Canal bank. The Vice-Chancellor said be had come to the conclusion, seeing that the pipe was undoubtedly in- tended and appropri ited for the purpose of carrying sewage from one point to another, that it was a sewer within the meaning of the Public Health Act. In his opinion the land over which it was proposed to lay the pipe was part of the Ship Canal wharf, and, as such, had been enclosed. Under these circum- stances be had come to the conclusion that the right of the Corporation to lay the pipe in the way they proposed was excluded by the clause in their Act which defined what a street was, because that Act prevented them from lacing down a sewer under any railway, canal, or navigation yard or wharf. Be therefore decided that the plaintiffs were entitled to an injunction to restrain the defendants from laying down the pipe from their sewage works to the canal wharf. It had been said that the pipe was a mode of embarkation. He could not so treat it. To regard a pipe 500 ft. long as an embarkation would be straining the word to an unnatural extent. With regard to costs he understood neither side asked for any, and therefore te made no mder.—Mr.Maberley (who appeared -for the Ship Canal Company) asked for a mandatory order for the removal of such portion of the pipe as had been laid down.—His Honour, in reply to Mr. Sutton, who appeared for the corporation, said they had no right under any portion of the company's land.—Mr. Sutton: Not even tlp part which has been dedicated as a road ? —His Honour : Sutton : It is there where we have laid it.—Mr.Maberley : But it was after notice of our right.--tlis Honour directed the issue of the manda- tory order, but said it should not be enforced for three months.

THE CHARGES AGAINST JABEZ BALFOUR.

The case against Jabez Spencer Balfour was opened by Mr. C. Mathews on behalf of the Treasury at Bow- st. Police Court on Thursday. It was stated that the charges would be of a three-fold character. In the first instarce the prisoner would be charged with obtaining £20,000 by false pretences from the House and Land Investment Trust. Then he would be charged for leaving the country within lour months of his bankruptcy with money and property o!' the value of over £2O. The third series of charges would deal with the alleged fraudulent misapplication of money belonging to the Building Securities Company, Limited. Formal evidence of the registration and winding up of the various companies was given, and the case was then adjourned until Monday.

RUNCORN AND THE CHEMICAL TRADE.

THE TRADES' AND LABOUR COUNCIL TAKES ACTION.

A meeting of the Runcorn Trades' and Labour Council was held on Tuesday evening, when it was unanimously decided to forward a memorial to Mr. Brock, chairman 1 of directors of the United Alkali Company. The, follow- ing is the text of the memorial which was forwarded last (Thursday; evening. "To the directors of the United Alkali Company,—Gentlemen, We beg most re- spectfully to approach your Board and earnestly solicit your careful consideration and the memorial of this Trades' Council. We hear with profound regret and alarm that it is the intention of your Board to close the whole of your works in the town of Runcorn. We are hopeful that on mkture consideration of all the ac- companying and surrounding circumstances, that our town will he saved from this threatened calamity. It is within the knowledge of your Board that the stoppage of these works would paralyse the trade of Runcorn, and bring innumerable homes, already impoverished by the trade depression and severity of the past winter, to direst poverty. This Council represents 11 district trades, having upwards of 1,503 members, all of whom are engaged or associated with this important industry, and would respectfully urge your Board, alike in the interests of the town of Runcorn and the workers in every branch of industry, to remove this threatened calamity. Truistingt ham the prompt action of your Board in the direction indicated, we remain, Dear Sir, and gentlemen, respectfully yours. President, George A. Kirkham ; secretary, Ellis Gatley."

o.oer NI BEFORE THE MA.GISTRATES.

A QUARREL OVER A CHILD. At the Wariengton Borough Court on Monday, Walter Ferrond, a stranger to the town, was charged with being drunk and di orderly in Buttermarket-street,on Saturday night. Alter the case had been proved, a man came forward, and said that on Saturday night, he was in Scotland-road. with his wife and child, looking at some bills. On looking round, he observed the prisoner walk- ing away with the child. He followed the prisoner, and demanded his child, but the prisoner only replied with some disgusting language. W,tness then told him that the child was his, but the prisoner replied that he would not give it up. The prisoner abs,.lutely refused to give it up until witness called a constable, and then he put the child on the ground. The prisoner wee drunk at the time.—The prisoner said that he never remembered the occurreace. The magistrates ordered him to leave the town at once. THEFT FROM A LODGER. Harriet Webb, 14, Cartwright-street, was charged at the Warrington Berough Court on Monday, with stealing a ladies' silver watch and chain, and also a gold keeper, valued £3, the property of Hannah Cannon, on March 30th. The Chief Constable said that Miss Cannon was at present engaged at Peacock's drapery establishment at Latchford and had been residing with the def mdant in Cartwright-street. The defendant was the wife of John Webb, tool maker. The complainant kept the articles above enutnerated,in her box,and on April 28 she missed them, ad on coming downstairs she told Mrs. Webb that she had missed her watch and chain. The defendant said that she had not taken them as she had a watch of her own Nothing more was said until Saturday of the mat- ter when it was reported to Moneelian, who then visited Mr.Briggs'pawnshop,Bank Qoay. Upon the artcles being described to Mr. Briggs, he recollected that he had taken the articles in pledge, and on being produced, the com- plainant identified them The defendant was then questioned by Monaghan, and she then admitted that she had pledged them, saving that her husband had been out of work so much lately. She also said that if she had known that the matter was going to be placed into the hands of the police, she would have brought them out of pledge. Mr. Hunt further said that he had reason to believe that the husband of the defendant had not been doing much work lately, and that the articles had , been pledged to buy the necessaries of life. He had 3en theecomnlainant and she was not wishful to press the case.—Miss Cannon was then called. but she said that she did not wish to say anything more than she had said to Mr. Monaghan.—Mr. Hunt said that there was no doubt that the act was done under necessity. Previous , to the present time, the defendant had been a very steady woman and her char ictcr had been very high—Thel bench decided to record a conviction against the defend- 1 ant on the charge, but inflicted no penalty. A BRUTAL FIIISBAND.—THE WIFE AS BREAD-WINNER. At the Warrington Borough Court, on Thursday,beforel Alderman Sutton (chairman), and Lieutenant-Colonel Ridgway, Peter Harrop, 44 Birehall-street, was charged with assaulting and beating his wife Ellen Flarrop on the 7th of May.—Mrs. Harrop, the complainant, stated that on the day in question the defendant came borne drunk and seized her by the neck with the intention she thought of strangling her. She broke loose and the de- fendant then picked up a chair and struck her with it on the forehead. On being questioned the complainant said her husband had to be drageed home nearly every Saturday night—The mother of complainant gave cor- roborative evidence and said the defendant was re,t steady man and did not, bring much money home, while the complainant worked hard at the mill and got some- times 15a. or £1 a week.—The Chairman said it was a disgraceful thing, and he often thought that the" cat " would be a good thing in that sort of ease —Defendant : Pm not as bad as they reckon I un.—Alderman Sutton : I don't know about that.—Defendant then promised to turn over a new leaf. Hia wife said that that was all she wanted ; she was willing to keep him AS long as he was out of work. The Chairman said they were dis- posed on the recommended/ n of his wife to be as lemient as poesible, and therefore they would adjourn the case ior a month and in the meantime the defendant would be hound over to keep the peace and appear when I called upon.

WARRINGTON BOROUGH COURT.

USING THREATS.—Mary Graham, 323, Winwick-road, was charged with having on the 27th April used threats to Margaret Emma Bibby ,305,W n wick- road. The com- plainant stated that on the day in question she heard a bother outside, and going to the door she was assailed with had language by the defendant, who also made use of a very vile threat. Complainant was in consequence afraid of suffering some bodily harm. CorrOborative evidence was given by a neighbour of complainant's, and defendant was bound over to keep the peace an I pay costs (Th.). Defendant said she would not pay one 42,1. and would go to gaol for seven days instead. MONDAY.—tlefore the Mayor (Councillor Jaillt'S Fair- dough), Alderman Harrison, Mr. F. Monks, and Mr.

THEFT OF VEGETABLES.—Sarah Ellen Ilanshury, 4, Robinaon's-yark was charged with stealing, in Dial-st., on Saturday qvening, a quantity of rhubarb and greens valued 31 , the property of Thomas Wit iamq, green- grocer, 18, :ntevenson-street. The Chief Constable stated that on Saturday evening, the complainant was in Dial. street, and left his truck while he went to the Old Crow. While he was there, he saw the defendant take some rhubarb, and greens, and run away. He ran after her and demanded rhem back, but she refused to give them up. Re could not go far after her as he had to go back and look after his truck, but he proceeded to the police

station where Detective Monahan was informed, and from the description given him, the defendant was arrested.—The complainant in supporting the chief con- stable's statement,said that the defendant had previously brought some watercress off him. Evidence was also given by Detective Monaghan.—The defendant, whose evidence was supported by Ellen McLoughlin denied that she stole the vegetables. She said that she paid for all that she had.—The magistrates, however, considered the case proved, and fined defendant 2. Bd. UNJUST SCALES. —Henry Ellison, 20, Percy-street,was charged with having a pair of scales which were unjust, on April 30:h. Mr. Place said that there were two pieces of lead on the scales which made them an ounce and eleven drams against the purchaser. He noticed that the handle of the scoop was missing, and he supposed that the lead was to counterbalance that. The scales were used for weighing potatoes. Mr. Place said that he did not think that the defendant was trying to cheat the public, but lie had admonished him time atter time. Defendant was fined 53. including costs.

THURSDAY—Before Alderman Sutton (chairman) and Lieutenant-Colonel Ridgway. THEFT.—Michael Burke, a young man, was charged with stealing from the shop of !tan Houghton, Butter- market-st., on Wednesday night,two pairs of stockings, valuv is. 31., her property. Detective Monaghan sta'ed that the s:ockings were seen safe outside Mrs. Houghton's shop in Buttermarket-at. on Wednesday evening, and were shortly afterwards missed. Informa- tion was given to witness, who in consequence arrested defendant and chaarged him with the theft, which he denied, and as the theft could not he brought IMme to him ho was released. It was not long,however,before the stockings were found pawned in a pawnshop in Mersey- at., where they had been pledged by defendant. The latter was again apprehended and admitted the offence. In court he said that he had been out of work for some time and had been tempted to steal.—The magistrates took a lenient view of the case and only recorded a conviction against the defendant, who was dismissed. THE EXAMINER, SAT URDA V, MAY 11, 1895.

RUNCORN PETIY SESSIONS.

MONDAY--Before Major Hazlehurst (chairman), Messrs. T. Rigby and 3. Taylor. LICENSING MATTERS.---The following transfer of licenses were granted :—Ship Inn, Heath-road, from James Hunt to Joseph Smith; Oidfellows Arms, Wel- lington-st., from John Shaw to Elias Hurst; Camden Hotel, Surrey-st., from John Fleming to Edward Orme ; Grapes Inn, from James Ditchfield to William Baker; Bird-in-Hand, from William Baker to Emma Field.— Alfred Craddock was granted permission to sell at the Fleetwood Arms, Church-st., and Alfred Clayden was also granted permission to sell at the Slop Inn. AN UNDICZZLED DOG.—William Sheen, 7 Porter et., was fined 6s. including costs for allowing a dog to beat large unmuzzled in Flalton-road, on the 27th April. DRONE AND DISORDERLY.—Ann Bostock, boatwoman, Vicar-st., Runcorn, for being drunk and disorderly in Hatton, on the 29th April, was fined Sc. including costs. SCHOOL ATTENDANCE CASES.—The following persons were fined for neglecting to send their children regularly to school. Mr. T. Bowyer, school attendance officer, prosecuted.—Thomas Hoxworth, Holloway; James Rose, Stoneeroft ; Ralph Ravenscrott, Lyon-st.; John O'Niel, Salkeld-square ; and John Gratten, Wickstone Hill. Warrants were issued in four cases where the de- fendants failed to answer the summons. NEW POOR RATE.—The magistrates affixed their signa- tures to a new poor rate of is. 81. in the £ for the town ship of Sutton, which is estim.ted to realise £545 18s.

WARRINGTON HIGHWAY BOARD.

The annual meeting of this Board was held in Bewsey Chambers on Wednesday afternoon. Mr. E. A. White, the deputy clerk, took the chair pro. tern., and there were also present: Captain Withington,Mossrs Royston, J. I-4110ton, Millington. J. Warburton, R. B. Matthews, Korfoor, J. Ball, B. Bate. P. J. Bate, R. T. Lowe, Ormrod,lClare,and W Thompion, (surveyor).

===== 0002601_18950511_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
ELECTION OF OFFICIALS.

Mr. MILLINGTO—N-1;14;(:)sd and Mr. ROYSTON seconded the re-election of Captain Withington as chairman for the ensuing year. This was carried unanimously. Captain Withington in thanking the members for re- appointing him alluded to the fact that he had been chairman of the Board since its form-ition in 1868. and he said that as their time expired in 1897, he hoped that he would be the chairman until the end.

Mr. Royston was re-elected senior vice-chairman on the motion of Mr. P J. BATE. seconded by Mr. CLARE, and Mr. James Flouditon was re-elected junior vice- chairman -n the motion of the Crtentatabr seconded by Mr. KEE FOOT.

FINANCE COMMITTEE..

- The following were a ppoint.-ct to the Finance Com- mittee for the ensuing year : the chairman, senior and junior vice-chairmen, and Messrs. J. Clare. El. Ba'e,and J. 1 Bate, R. T. Lowe, J. Kerfoot, J. Ball, J. Warburton.

THE AUDIT.

The CLERK SA id that the audit had been held on the previous Ttiursday and had passed off very satisfactor- ily. SURVEYOR'S ESTIMATES.

The Surveyor then submitted his estimates for the en- suing year which were based upon the estimate of an 81. rate. The total amount required was £4.851 15s. 8d and the following contributions will be required from the various town.hips : Burtonwood. £589 lie. 4d.; Cuerdley. £193 13s. 41.; Cutcheth, £5OB 12s ; Great Sankey, £262 1.1. e. 4,1.; Houghton, £7B 145.; Kenyon, £338 125. 61.; Little Sankey, £292 18s. 81.; Lowton, £352 B+. 81.; P..nketh, £272 t. 581.; Poulton,. £2Ol. 9 441 ; Rixton. £266 Ba.; C,rof t, £156 as. 81.; Winwick, £4BB Bs. 81. ; Woolston, £149 Bs. ; total, £4,150 17s. 21. The maintenance of secondary roads was estimated at £1,700 ; district roads, £1,445 ; improvements, £1.043; salaries, £463 14s ; and general expenses £2OO The Imprivements for the various townships were estimated to be, Burton- wood, £3O; Cuerdley £6B; Culcheth, £155; Great San- key, £6O; Houghton, £6O; Kenyon, £5O; Lowton, £lOO included in the second,ry roads estimates; Pen- keth, £9O; Poulton, £100; 11,xton, £100; Croft, £80; Winwick, £140; Woolston, £110; t,tad £1,043. It was estimated that £B5O would be returned from the County Council.—Mr. ROYSTON objected to an 81. rate. He proposed as an amendment that tie rate be the same as last year, namely 7d.—The CLERK said that a 71. rate would probably meet the expenditure although it would be cutting it very fine.—Mr. KERFO .T seconded Mr. Royston's motion.—Mr. P. J. BATE moved an amend- ment that the rate he 81. in the £ as suggested by the Surveyor.—Mr. ORMROD seconded the amendment, and upon the resolution and amendment being put to the vote, there voted for an 81. rate, Messrs. H. Bate, P. J. Bate,Ormrod, Clare, Houghton, and Ball—for a 7d. rate, Messrs. Royston, Lowe, Millington, Warburton, and Kerfoot.—An Bd. rate will therefore be levied for the ensuing year.

BIRTHS AND DEATHS.

During the year ending December 31st, 1894, 661 births and 359 deaths were regilvered as having occurred within the Rural District ; these figures, compared with those ,4 last year, show a decrease of 62 births and a further neerease of 81 deaths. But these 359 deaths include all those which have occurred within Dutton Workhouse, amounting in all to 26 ; 15 of these are des- cribed in the deaths returns as belonging to Runcorn Urban District : deducting these from the total 4 359 deaths, we arrive at a corrected total, for the Rural District, of 344. Of these no less than 39 formed the onbi.-cs of Coroners' enquiries (the same number as in 1893), while 6 were returned as " uncertified," or "'No Medical Attendant." Of the births, 339 were males, and 329 females; of the deaths, 194 were males, and 164 females. The birth rate for the year 1891, was 29 7 per 1000 per annum of estimated population,compared with 30 8 per 1000 in 1893. The average birth rate for the preceding 10 years being 30 1 per 1000. the birth rate for 1894 is thus slightly below the average.

===== 0002601_18950511_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
ZYMOTIC MORTALITY' .

Compared with last year, there is a very distinct im- provement, nly 48 deaths having occurred from all the zymotic diseases, corrinared with 70 in 1893; and of these 48 deaths in 1894, 15, or almost one-thir were caused by whooping cough, and all occurred in children under rive ears of age. Smallpox is entirely absent from the lit, while diarrhcea. which•in 1893 carried off 35, caused only 9 deaths in 1894, 6 of which were chil- dren under 5 years of age. Influenza, which appeared again in the district in 1894. accounted for 6 deith, compared with 7 de-ithe in 1893, 17 in 1892, 19 in 1891, and 6 in 1890, when it first appeared amongst us.

===== 0002601_18950511_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
OTHER DISEASES.

The other diseases included phthisis—causing 21 deaths, 2 of which were in children under syears oi age, and 19 in persons over 5 yea's of age, and yielding 5 8 per cent. of total deaths, or a death-rate of .9 per 1,000 per annum of estimated population; bronchitis, pneumonia, and pleurisy—causing 51 deaths, of which 26 occurred in persons under 5 years of age, and 25 in persons over 5 years of age; yielding 142 ner cent, of total deaths, or a death-rate of 2.18 per 1,000 per annum of estimated population; heart disease, &.—causing 36 deaths. all of them in persons over 5 years of age; yield- ing 10.0 prr cent. of total deaths, with a death-rate of 1.5 per 1,000 per annum of estimated population; in- juries, accidents, and violence—causing 27 deaths of which 3 were in persons of under 5 years of age, and 24 in persons of 5 years of age and upwards; yielding 7.5 per cent, of total deaths, with a death-rate of 1•1 per 1,000 per annum of esti- mated population; all other diseases causing 176 deaths, of which 56 occurred in persons under 5 years of age, and 120 in persons of 5 years and upwards ; yielding 49 per cent, of the total deaths, with a death- rate of 7.9 per 1,000 per annum of estimated popula- tion.

INFECTIOUS DISEASES —202 cases of infectious diseases wer” notified in 1894, compared with, in 1893. •SOB cases; In 1892, 124 cases; in 1891, 108 cases; in 1890, 105 eases. Scarlet fever invaded 20 out of the 36 townships of the rural district, but the main incidence of the disease was mcentrated on Kingsley (49 cases), and Weston (35 cases). The con. inued spread of the disease in the former of these townships, in spite of every precaution, led to the closing of the schools, when the outbreak rapidly subsided. In the case of Weston, it was decided to withhold the order for the closing of the schools, as sources of intection appeared to be probable outride school ; the disease shortly afterwards abated. Com- menting on this Dr. Adams says: Again, as I have repeatedly pointed out, if an Isolation Hospital had been ready for the reception of one or two of the earlier cases, the outbreak (in the cases of Kingsley and Weston) might have been confined to very narrow limits, while the ab,ence of any arrangements, sanctioned by this Authority, to utilme their steam disinfector, led to the continued infective power of clothing, bedding, .kc., being established, long after the patients had fully recovered. I understand, however, that the committee appointed to deal with my rezommendations on this point, an-. about to submit a report, which, if adopted, will carry out my recommendations on this matter." With regard to the sanitary arrangements z..f the fol- lowing townships the Medical Officer reported as follows :

- APPLETON The contemplated sewerage extension referred to in last year's report has been effected this year, and moreover has been added to ; there has been a considerable addition of new houses in Stockton Meath ; in fact this part of the township is increasing rapidly. Seven cases of scarlet fever, and two of typh.Jid fever, occurred during 1891, but no deaths. FRODSHAId : Frodsham has a water supply at last. 'the water scheme is now completed, and many houses are already connected to it. A further loan of money has been raised, to enable the water scheme to reach more distant outlying parts of the district, which is a matter much to he desired. Re could only repeat, with emphasis, his regret that Frodsham Lordship hiS decided at present to forego the benefits pf this scheme, by abstention from participation in it. Fredsham contri- buted 6 cases of scarlet fever, 6 of diphtheria, 1 of typhoid fever, and 5 of erysipelas, to the list of notified cases; while its zymotic -death roll comprised 5 deaths from measles, and 1 from diarrlicea, FRODSHAII LORDSHIP: There were 4 cases of scarlet fever, 1 of typhoid, and 1 of erysipelas were notified ; zymotic deaths were from—typhoid fever, 1; measles, 1; and whooping cough, 3. HALTON : There is no alteration in the water supply to "'TheCom•non." Perhaps the new Parochial Committee will he able to effect an alteration. _ _

FIELSBY : The consideration of improved sanitation of a public character remains in abeyance. There has been little done as to providing a water scheme. One care of diphtheria, one of typhoid fever, and one of erysipelas, were notified; the zymotic deaths were, oar! from diphtheria, and one frem whooping cough. MOORS : The :sewer in Moss-lane has undergone a further extensions; otherwise there has been nothing to record. One case of typhoid fever was notified, and died.

WHITLEY INFERIOR AND SUPERIOR After 1893, when 37 cases of scarlet fever, with one death, occurred, it is satisfactory to know that only one case was reported in each of these townships diiring 1894. The only zvmotic death occurred in Whitley In- ferior, from whooping cough. WESTON ; While Westan has figured promin- ently in the matter of nptified infectious diseases (comprising 35 cases of scarlet fever, 5 of (liph,heria. 4 of typhoid fever, and 1 of erysipelas),it has escaped with only one zymotin death from scarlet•fever. There is yet room for great im- provement as regards water pressure, and he would di- rect their attention to this matter during the coming year. As suggested last year, " the supply should be made continuous, by enlargement of the mains by the Runcorn Commissioners, which I believe would be in accordance with their Act of Parliament."

A report by tho inspector of Nuisances (Mr. J. Far- rington) is also given.

O'DONOVAN ROSSA. IN rHE HOUSE OF

COMMONS. SINGULAR INCIDENT.

An almost somnolent House of Commons was on Wed- nesday afternoon startled out of its placid endurance of an afternoon's debate on a question on which every member has already made up his mind, by a voice, rich in brogue, suddenly resounding from the Strangers' Seats under the gallery. For a moment it was supposed by thitse who failed to realise that this did not proceed from the Nationalist benches, that an Irish member was interrupting Mr. Dunbar Barton in an exclamation he had risen to make respecting something attributed to him by the Chief Secretary. " Mr. Speaker," weee the words that were hurtled across the chamber, "an assas- sin's blow has been dealt me in this House." By this time members realised that the dearest privileges of the House were being outraged by the audacity of some straneer endeavouring to make known his grievance. The invasion was resented by loud cries of " Order" and Stranger." Bel ore the Deputy-Sergeant-at-Arms could reach the interrupter from the front, and before the attendants could assail him in the rear, the ' voice went on, " A stain has been put upon My name, and I have come down to this House in order to say --" So far it got, but no farther,for the guardians of 11 ow and order were by this time within reach of the trespasser, and the rest of his observations remain lost in oblivion. He very quietly obeyed the order to with- draw, and stood in the Central Hall placidly awaiting developments, and possibly not wholly undesirous of having, for purposes of fame in America, the honours of the Clock 'rower, for by this time it was very generally known that it was Mr. O'Donovan Rossa who had created this diversion. Inspector Horsley arrived in the Central Hall almost as soon as the intruder. No heroic martyrdom was, however, reserved for the latter. Act- on the instructions of Mr. Gosset, the inspector techni- cally removed the flouter of Parliamentary manners from the sacred precincts. There was no need for vio- lence. Roma placidly walked beside his captor dawn the corridor, across the outer 'octagon, and dawn St. Stephen's Hall, until he was delivered into the street, directions being given to all the constables passed on the way that he was not to be readmitted. While outside, apparently deliberating where next to go, O'Donovan Hosea was joined by Mr. William Red- mond and one or two members of Parliament. From the statements made to them, it appears that the grievance which he had taken this sensational method of ventilat- ing was not anything that had occurred in debate, but a letter written by Mr. Labouchere some six years ago. This be produced, amid a crowd of other documents, from his pocket,in justification of the original step taken for his vindication. The exhibition thus created has been the principal topic of discussion in the lobbv.There is every reason to believe that Rossa went deliberately to the House in order to advertise himself. He was ad- mitted on an admission order signed by Mr. Edward Barry, member for Mid Cork.

MESSRS. FAIRCLOITGH AND THE SHIP CANAL.

In the Queen's Bench Divisional Court last Friday, betore Mr. Justice Charles and Mr. Justice Wright, an application was heard in the matter of Messrs. J4nies Farclough ano Sons, millers, Cooper's W harf, Warring- ton, and the Manchester Ship Canal, arising out of an arbitration between the parties. The question which arose was as to the construction of section 82. sub-section 3, of the Manchester Ship Canal Act of 1885, it being agreed between both sides that the arbitration should be suspended, and a case stated for the opinion of the Divisional Courts

Mr. Justice Wright: Is this question one of great importance ? Mr. Joseph Walton : It is, my lord, one of very great importance ? Mr. Justice Wright : If so, it is very unfortunate that in this course of procedure you have no appeal. Mr. Cripps, Q.C., said both parties considered the matter, and they agreed that the question sheuld be raised and decided in this way so that both parties would take their chance as to the right of appeal. Mr. Walton, continuing, said that in addition to Coopers Wharf, belonging to his clients, there was a number of other wharves and docks adjacent, such as West Bank Dock, Sankey Canal, Widnes Dock, in addi- tion to wharves belonging to Messrs. Gossage and Cs. and Messrs. Hutchinson and Co. Sub-section 3 of section 82 of the Act stated that if at any time during or after the construction of the works authorised by the Act the river Mersey from the most easterly boundary of Widnes downwards should become so changed as to hinder or prevent such vessels as were at the time of the passing of the Act able to enter and leave the Widnes Doek, the Westbank Dock at Widnes, the Sankey Canal and Ditton Brook, and to navigate between Whines and any other place or places lower down the Mersey I ruin continu- ing so to do in as free and ample a manner as they had hitherto done" the company, unless they can prove to the satisfaction of an arbitrator,to be appointed as here- inafter mentioned,that such change is not to be assigned to the said works,shall at the request of the Local Board of Widnes or of any aggrieved Corporation or persons, from time to time keep open, by dredging or otherwise, convenient communications between the said Sankey Canal and Ditton Brook and the wharves adjoining ,the entrances to the Sankey Canal belonging, or reputed to belong' to William Gossage and Sons, J. Hutchinson and Co., William Coopex and Co., and William Wright, respectively, and the locks and entrances of the company or one of them, so as to enable such vessels to enter and leave the Canal at the times they have hitherto been able to navigate the said river Mersey." The case had arisen out of a claim made by Messrs. Fairclough, who were the owners of one of the wharves mentioned in the sub- section for damages they had sustained in consequence ot the navigation from their wharf down the river being impeded and by reason of the Canal Co. not having done hat which they were required to do,namely,the opt cling up of convenient communication by dredging or other- wise between Cooper's Wharf and the other locks and entrances. He contended that vessels could not now navigate as well as they could before from Coeper's Wharf down the river in the Liverpool direction. Next he said they could not leave Widnes Duck and navi- gate to places down the river, namely, to Cooper's Wharf. His main contention was that the navigation of the Mersey between Widnes and places' lower down the river had been interfered with,anl that he su hmitted brought him within the section. He contended that the Ship Canal Company were bound by that section to keep the navigatisin open in the terms of sub-section 3. As to the question of compensation he consented to its being left over until the construction of the section shall be determined., . _

Mr. Cripps, Q.C., for the Manchester Ship Canal, ad- mitted that there was a considerable deal of diffieulty in construinz the section. Tne question was Ofls 01' con- siderable importance, however, to the Manchester Strip Canal Company. It was not contended that there was any interference with the main navigation of the river Mersey. In this case there was but a little local silting, and in the case of such local interference remedies were provided by sub-section 1 of section 82. But if the owners of wharfs, in consequeoce of ocal silting could come to the Court and ask for such remedy as was meant only for cases in which there was interference with the main naviga- tion down to Liverpool the conseqvences would he, that it would necessite the expenditure of an enormous sum instead of a mere trifling expenditure. In fact it would throw an enormous burden upon the Manchester Ship Canal Company. In reply to further objections by their lordships, Mr. Cripps said that if the arbitration went on it meant a very long and expensive inquiry, and the calling of a long list cf expert witnesses. Therefore they both thought that this would be the right time, if possible,to have the matter decided.

Mr. Justice Charles : If we assent to any portion of Mr. Walton's claim the arbitrator would make no award about it. If possible you might ask the Lord Chief Justice to constitute a Divisional Court, say of five judges or of three, so that the case could be disposed of. Supposing my learned brother and myself disagreed it would be distinctly a waste of time, and there being no appeal I suppose my judgment would prevail. Mr. Cripps said if that were the view of their lordships Mr. Walton should apply for a larger court and the case might stand over. Mr. Justice Charles said he would speak to the Lord Chief Justice upon the [natter, and eventually the case was allowed to stand over until Monday for arrange- ments to be made for the constitution of a larger divi- sional caurt to try the question.

INQUEST AND VERDICT.

'.:,'t,T-!".:.!-,'.f.'-...,,;;,7,..':•..f.i.,'.r;h4-. ~..,...-._;,,,,,... ".',':':-, .:.-..,..:.:':....'-.‘.'7;'.1'.-...7..'''t.•;•-•.'1:.4:',,:'-'

Mr.S. Brighouse held an inguem last Friday afternoon at the Black Horse, Sankey Bridges, on the body of William Edmonds, aged 19 years, son of Matthew Ed- monds, 19,Turner-et., who was killed on the London and North-Western Railway (low lev.11) at Ponketli Hall crossing on Tuesday, by the 3.22 trsin from Widnes. Mr. Fenna was present on bAlalf of the Lmidon and North-Western Railway Company, and Mr. A. Browne was present on behalf of the relatives of the deceased.

_ Matthew Edmonds said his son was a rag and bone gatherer. He left home early on Tuesday morning with a donkey and cart. He never saw his son again alive. . _ . .

Catherine Caveney said that she was near the lino at the time the deceased was killed. She saw him knocked down by the trate, and be was killed instantly. Sue could not tell how far the train was when deceased got on the line. He had been to a house ou the other side of the line, and when he was crossing he had some chalk in his hand, and this was found on the line after he was killed.—By Mr. Browne : Anyone who wished to get to the house where deceased had been to, had to cross the line. A man used to be at the crossing, but elle had not seen one lately. Hannah Thompson said that she lived on the side of the grossing, near the canal. To get. to her house from the highway, she had to cross the railway. Deceased came to her house at 3.25 on Tuesday afternoon and she bought some chalkutrom him. Soon afterwards in consequence of the passenger train stopping, she went out, and found deceased lying on the rails dead.—By Mr. Fenno, : She was in charge of the crossing when her husband was at work. It was an occupation level crossing. Mr. Penne was proceeding to cross-examine with regard to the right of crossing the line, when the coroner interrupting said that he would hold that the man had a right to cross the line. As to passing and re- passing, it was only reasonable to suppose that anybody who supplied greengroceries and rubbing stones had to go across the crossing to get to the house.—Bv Mr. Browne : She had red and green flags in the house, but she never used them except when the men were attend- ing to the rails. William Hardy, 10 Percy-st., engine driver,.said that he was in charge of the 3.27 train from Fiddler's Ferry. On leaving the Ferry station in the direction of Warring- ton, his mate was engaged with the fire, and witness was on the" six•foot " side of his engine. On getting near the crossing he blew the whistle of the engine, as engine drivers were instructed to do when approaching level crossings. Immediately afterwards he 3.1. W a young man go across the line looking towards another train which was coming from Warrington. fie never I seemed to take any notice of the other train, and the buffer of witness's engine struck him and knocked him to the ground. Witness .at Once stopped the train. When first seen, the deceased was not a dozen yards from the engine, and witness could not possibly have stopped the train in time. The train was going about 20 miles an hour at the time. The train of which wit- ness was in charge, crossed the crossing before the train from Warrington. The Coroner said that the case ought not to take up much time. The railway company when they formed the line upon which the accident had taken place had greater power than they had now. No doubt at the present time, if they wore making the railway,the legis- lature would see that more protection was given to passengers than they did in former times. They.fnust take it that at level crossings, by Act of Parliament the Company had a right to pa.-,s arid repass with their trains. The public had in a limited degree also a right to cross over the line. This crossing was an occupation road crossing and he thought that the man had a perfect right to go across the line. But there was no possible obligation to the Company to keep a caretaker there. It appeared to him that; the deceased's attention was di- verted by the train from Warringtoe, and that be thus did not see the other train coming. There was a large number of accidents elsewhere owing to the same thing.

The jury returned a verdict ot •7 Accidental death." Mr. Grimshaw the foreman of the jury said that within two miles of the place of the accident, t her.. were five level crossing?,and he complained that the whistles of the engines were not blown as often as they ought to be, but the Coroner said that in the present ease, the evidence showed that the driver had blown his whistle.

THE RETURN OF JABEZ BALFOUR.

IN THE DOCK.

The Tartar Prince with Jaber Spencer Balfour on board arrived at Southampton early on Monday. A Customs launch at once went out to the steamer, and Balfour, in thee! arge of Inspectors Froest and Moore, was taken from the vessel to the dockyard. A first-class compart- ment on the ordinary traiu to Waterloo station, London, had been reserved for the prisoner and his custodians, and they reached London shortly after ten. Contrary to public expectation, Balfour and his escort left the train at Vauxhall station,and were driven thence to Bow-street Police Court. He was taken betore Sir John Bridge soon after twelve o'clock, and the Hon. Mr. Cuffe, who prose- cuted on behalf of the Treasury, at once asked for a remand until Thursday. Mr. J. O'Connor (formerly a member of Parliament), who appeared for the prisoner, objected to sueh a short remand, and stated that he had no information as to what the charge against the prisoner was, although an appli- cation had been made to the authorities. Mr. Cuffe said that was not true, whereupon Mr. O'Connor said he must have been wrongly instructed. After further dis- cmsion it was agreed that only sufficient evidence should be given to justify a remand till Thursday, when the case will be opened but no evidence taken. Sube- quently Mr. O'Connor raised the question whether the , charges made against the prisoner now were ,identical with those made at Salta and Buenos Ayres. Sir J. Bridge said the prisoner was present, and must be tried ,by the warrants grinted by the Court. A remand to , Thursday was then taken, and the prisoner was re- moved to Holloway prison. From the statements made by passengers on the Tartar Prince, it appears that Bal- four gave no trouble on the voyage and was a general favourite on board. Every precaution was, of course, taken against any attempt to commit suicide. On leav- ing the steamer he thanked the captain for the way in which he had been treated, and said, "1 have a lot of trouble, but it will be seen that I have not done wrong."

SUPPOSED SUICIDE OF A YOUTH AT

SAiNiKEY BRIDGES. SAD STORY.

An inquest was held last Friday at the Black Horse, Sankey Bridges, by Mr. S. Brighouse, unon the body of William Jowett, aged 19 years, son of Thomas Jowett, 295, Liverpool-road, Sankey Bridges, whose body was found in the Sankey Canal, on Thursday. Martha Ann Jowett, said that deceased w.,,s her son and was a whitesmith, employed at Mr.. Garland's shop. On Monday, he came home about a quarter to on. for his dinner. She told him that she was getting his little brother away for the good ot his health, and it was a great expense. At that, he looked anxiously at her and said " What about my tyre. I want it for the parade." She told him that he 'spoke harshly, and asked him to remember who she wa ,111111 he looked very wild. She told him he could not ' his tyre. He then said that he must have it, and re- peated that three times. She told him to remember that she was his mother and he should not speak so harshly to her. She said that he had had a lot of trouble and that no doubt hurt him. He then got wildly on his legs, I and she told him to sit down but he Said ho would go out. She and her daughter down, held him in the house for half an hour, and another daughter went for his father. When his father came, he told deceased thst he must not kick up a bother like that. H. said he would go out and 11 would be out of that bother. He then went out. 11 and he never camo back alive.. He never said exactly that he would take away his life, although she was frightened at times he would do so. He had a scar on his head caused by being cut by a cart when he was seven years of age, and since then he soon lost his tem- per. He had had some trouble a short time ago, and that had preyed very heavily upon his mind. Thomas Jowett deceased's father, said that on Mon- slay he was sent for from his work and when he got home, his wife had fainted. He asked his son what he had been doing, and deceased then asked his mother t ) forgive him, hut sre could not speak. He then wanted to go out, and witness said, " Let him go out. He will soon be back." Deceased then went out, and wit- ness saw his son go along the cabal bank towards Butter- milk bridge, and thought that he was perhaps going to a friend's house in Lovely-lane. Fle had been very excit- able during the past few months. . .

Benjamin Welding, waterman, said that on Thursday afternoon the deceased's mother came to him,and a,•lre-d him to drag the canal for she was afraid her son WAS in it. He accordingly got assistance and dragged the canal as far as the railway arches over which the Cheshire lines went, and found deceased there at 7.50 p.m. He was fully dressed with the exception of his cap. P.S. Devlin informed the coroner and jury that he had had a conversation with Mr. Garland. the &ceased's employer' and he gave him a very good character. He said that he had much improved in his work of' late. _

The Coroner said that this was a very pad case to in- quire into. There was no dount that the deceased had had some dispute before leaving the house, but so far as the evidence went, nothing, was known about him from then until he was found in the canal. It would be for the, jury to say whether it was sufficient to return a verdict of found drowned, as there was no evidence to show how he had got into the water. If they found a verdict of suicide, they would have to say what state of mind he was in at the time.

After a short consultation, the jury returned a ver- dict of "Found drown,A." as there was no direct evi- dence as to how decPased Rot into the water.
