ARMENIANS ASK FOR JUSTIUE.

Sir,—! would ask your correspondent of last week if he meant by" peace at any price" that he would sell his personal liberty or liberty of conscience or even speech for the sake of peace. The Rev. John Yonge only uttered a just and Christian like indignant protest which might have been justly uttered from every church and endorsed by every Government in the world. For we know sufficiently of Turkey to be aware that it is only by the sword of justice that she may be prevailed upon from killing Christians in cold blood. The gentleman would accuse Mr. Yonge of being political in his sermon because he straightforwardly and unflinchingly spoke his feelings and sentiments on this great subject, before, I hope,a living,and I am sure an admiring congregation; and relieved his mind of what might otherwise have seemed indifference and callousness. Your correspon- dent next accuses Mr. Gladstone, who is now in private life, of making war on Egypt in class interests, but I would point out that a Government is bound to give protection to any of the Queen's subjects providing their claim is legal.— Yours truly,

DEATH AFTER ANTI-TOX,N,

===== 0002601_18950511_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
A READER.

Sir,—As the anti-toxin treatment is now attracting so much public attention, I think the following extract from the " Lancet " of April 20th, cannot fail to interest your readers :—" The death of two persons immediately following the injection of so-called anti-toxin excites a profound interest in the medical profession. The facts are as follows : A boy from the country while visiting his uncle in Brooklyn developed diphtheria ; as he gra- dually grew worse under treatment the physician ob- tained a bottle of anti-toxin. An injection of this fluid was made, and the boy died three hours after. On the following day a seventeen-year-old daughter of the uncle complained of a sore throat. The physician ob- tained another bottle of the anti-toxin, and it was ad- ministered. After receiving the injection the girl said she felt a tickling sensation all through her body. She then fell on the lounge in convulsions, and was dead in ten minutes, despite the physician's efforts to save her. Her face turned t4ack almost immediately after receiv- ing the fluid. The physician states that the girl's death I was a mystery to him, and he could only account for it by crediting it to some powerful poison in the bottle of anti-toxin. He had used the remedy for some time with gratifying results, and always got it at the same place. The colour was the same as that he had used, being pink. He had never "iwitnessed such a terrible death. There had been a mistake somewhere in bottl- ing the fluid. The amount injected was between two and three drachms, which came, in each bottle. The bottle was put up in Germany, and bears date of ship- ment, January 30-h last. The necropsy revealed nothing abnormal." These I may add, are not isolated cases. We are unfortunately hearing from all sides of fatali- ties. Under the circumstances it behoves all to consider well before they have recourse to so dangerous a "cure."—Yours truly, • Bellgarth, Hendon, May 4th, 1895.

INTERESTING MA RRIAGE AT RUNCORN.

A wedding, in which a great deal of interest was taken, was solemnized at the Primitive Methodist Chapel, Runcorn, on Wednesday morning. The con- tracting parties were Mr. Joseph W. Coombs, son of the late Rev. J. Coombs, Pi Motive Methodist minister' and Miss Harriet A. Broadhurst, daughter of Mr. Isaac Broadhurst, coal merchant, Ashridge-st., Runcorn. A large congregation assembled in the chapel to witness the ceremony. As the parties entered, Mr. Shakespeare, of Liverpool, played a suitable voluntary on the organ. The officiating clergyman was the Rev. G. Rennison, of Newtown (brother in-law of the bridegroom), and he was assisted by the Revs. Thomas Bramall and H. R. Didcock. The Pervice was fully choral and the two hymns "The Voice that breathed o'er Eden," and "Saviour let Thy sanction rest," were sung. The bride, who was given away by Mr. Broadhurst, was attired in a dress of fawn honeycomb cloth, and was adorned with a wreath of orange blossoms with tulle veil. She ; wore a gold bangle bracelet, the gift of the bride- groom, and carried a shower bouquet. There were six bridesmaids delicately attired in dresses of geranium pink with hats to match. Each was wear- ing a gold brooch, presented by the bridegroom, and carried a beautiful bouquet of marguerites. The bridesmaids were : Miss Coombs, Runcorn, sister of the bridegroom ; Miss Billingsley, Miss G. Stubbs, Balton Brook, cousin of the bride ; Miss Birlow, Liverpool, cousin of the bride ; Miss A. Bramall, and H. R. Didcock, and Mr. T. Reynolds. The choir at the church and a number of friends, in all about one hundred were provided with tea in the afternoon. EARLESTONVN AND NEWTON.

OCCASIONAL COURT. At the Occasional Court held on Monday at the Earlestown Police Station. a tramp named Robert Porter was charged with being drunk inEarlestown on Saturday night. He was discharged on promis- ing to leave the town. VULCAN WESLEYAN SUNDAY SCHOOL. Special services in connection with the above were held in the school 211 Sunday afternoon and evening. Special hymni were sung by the choir and children under the leadership of Mr. H.Grundy. The services were conducted by the Rev. J. Bart- ley, who preached appropriate sermons. The collec- tions amounted to £4 7s. id., in aid of the school funds.

SCY3IAL EVENING. In connection with the teachers of St. John's Sunday School, Earlestown, a social evening was held in the Mission Hall, Earlestown, on Saturday evening. About 70 persons were present, the band of the Earlestown Orchestral Society was also in atteudance and played several selections of music, for dancing. Refreshments were provided, and a very pleasant evening was spent. The proceedings were in aid of a summer picnic.

EARLBSTOWN SOCIIL CYCLING CLUB.

This club has 80 far been pretty busy in the mat- ter of runs, and although the musters have not been what might be called numerous, the expeditions have been decidedly pleasant. On Saturday an out- ing was made to Manchester, and on Sunday atter- noon when there was a large turnout the destina- tion was Rainhill. The day was beautifully tine, and although a trifle hot, the run was thoroughly enjoyed. On Sunday a run will be made to South- port. Mr. T. Waring, of Oxtord-st.,is the secretary, from whom anyone wishing to join can obtain all information.

LANCASHIRE FOOTBALL A SSOCIATION. —StiSPEN- SION OF PLAYERS.

A sub-committee of the above was held at the Patten Arms Hotel, Warrington. Mr. Woolfall, Blackburn, presiding. An inquiry was made into a case of misconduct on April 24 in the Warrington and District League match (first division) between Earlestown Victoria and Parr Victoria, played otC the Earlestown Football ground. The referee (Mr. John Mather) reported that during the last quarter of an hour the Parr goalkeeper and full backs were grossly atta:dted by the spectators, sods, sticks, and caps being thrown at them. he referee himself was severely threatened, and otherwise roughly handled, and Aildridg.S. an Earlestown Victoria player, struck a Parr man. The scene was further continued after the match, and it was only through the assistance of the Earlestown Football Com- mittee that the referee and the visiting team were able to get home with what might be called safety. Ater full inquiry and deliberation, it was decided that the Earlestown Victoria Club be suspended till October 31st and Alldridge (player) till November 30th. The Earlestown Football ground was not interfered with, as the Victoria Club were proved to he a distinct club, and only used the ground on suffrance EARLESTOWN M ISSION SUNDAY SCHOOL.

During the winter months Mr. Dring, town miv- sionary, held a series of week night services in Houghton-st.. which were of an encouraging char- acter, many children and adults attending who had not been in the habit of .being present at any place of worship. From this beginning he was encouraged to start a Sunday school, which he did at the Co- operative Hall, and so far it has been of a successful character. Mr. A. Morris has taken up the onerous duty of superintendent, and the school, which in- cludes children of the poorer classes, is now getting into fair working order. On Saturday night,in order to assist the funds required to work the school, a service of song entitled, " Christie's old organ," was given. Solos were sung by Mrs. Dring and Mr. Brown,and the narrative was read by kir.W. Plant, of Havelock. The subject was illustrated by the aid of the limelight, the lantern being worked by Mr. Lawson. The harmonium was presided over by Master S. Dring.—On Sunday special services were held in the Co-operative Hall, in the after- noon and evening. Special. hymns and solos were sung by the children. Mr. Brown sang the anthem " I will arise," and Master H. Bridge sang "God will take care of you." The afternoon service was con- ducted by Mr.E. Weaver, and in the evening by Mr. W.Robinson The combined efforts realiz-td the sum of £3 15s..which will be of considerable assist- ance to Mr. Dying in his work in connection with the Sunday School. NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLI4ISTOWN. (BY AN OCCASIONAL CONTRIBUTOR:.) I would remind my readers that the anniversary ,ervices in connection with the Earlestown Wes- leyan Sunday School will be held on Sunday. In addition to the ordinary services, there will be a ' sacred cantata in the chapel in the afternoon, at which the choir and several of the scholars in addi- tion to the band of the Earlestown Orchestral Society,lwill be present. The services are in aid of the funds of the Sunday school, and it is to be hoped the public will support such a worthy object.

It is well-known that during this week a high wind has prevailed in this neighbourhood, and in consequence has gathered and blown in blinding clouds along the thoroughfares, large quantities of dust. 'this has caused great inconvenience amongst the inhabitants, but, the authorities have to a cer- tain extent ignored the wants of the people. We have a water cart, and I must admit that it has dis- tributed water on the roads in some parts of the town, but other parts have been left severely alone. I suppose we all pay rates, and are entitled to an equal share of the patronage of the water cart, but whatever may be the cause, the inhabi- tants as I have said, have had to put up with the dust. I don't think this is scarcely fair, and where houses abut on the pavement, I certainly consider the authorities should see that the dust is kept down.

The Council admitted at their meeting on Mon- day that the water supply was being well main- tained, but complained that it was rather sandy. The cause of this was explained by Mr. Emmett, but they have appointed a sub-committee to wait upon Mr. Webb in respect to the water question, If the supply is good and sufficient for the wants of the township, I cannot conceive what on earth they want to Lee Mr. Webb about. I think the best thing they can do is to let well alone, and rest , content.

I am pleased, and no doubt all other ratepayers will be equally gratifi,d to learn that the rate., for the current year are to be the same as last year. At any rate there is no increase, and however much we may wish for a reduction, still it is pleasing to know that we shall not have to pay more. This is a creditable reflection on the old Board of Com- missioners.

INTERESTING ACTIoN BY A• HAYDOCK SCHOOLMISTRESS.

A CASE OF WRONGFUL DISMISSAL.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes, Charlotte Tait, schoolmistress, Haydock, sued Mr. Theodore Drayton Grimke-Drayton, and Mr. W. S. Barrett, the managers of the Blackbrook British School, Haydock, for £45 163. Bd. for salary as headmistress of the above school, and for share of the Government grant, and salary in lieu of notice, and damages for wrongful dismissal. The particulars of the claim were as follows :—Five months salary up to June 27th at £.50 per annum, £2O 163. Bd. ; one quarter share of the government grant for the year ending in March, £2O ; and three months proportion of the grant up to June 27, £5. Mr. Jordan, barrister, Manchester, instructed by Mr. H. J. Long,ton, appeared for the plaintiff, and Mr. U. E. Allen, Wigan, defended. Mr. Jordan, in opening the case, said that the facts of the case were that the school in Whioll Miss Tait filled the position of headmistress was a colliery school near Newton-le-Willows. Certain gentlemen who owned the colliery where the managers of the whole affairs in con- nection with the school. Miss Tait was engaged on May 23rd, 1887, at a salary of £5O per annum, and she was also to receive a quarter of the government grant. She Was engaged by Ruth Evans, who was the correspon- dent for the directors, and received and answered all communications with the Education Department,and also the general briefness of the schnol. She entered upon her duties on June 27, 1887, and held her position until the commencement of the present year, when matters arose which gave rise to the present action. Some time ago the colliery company was turned into a limited liability company,but so far as Miss Tait was concerned matters went on just as usual. There was no notice of any description ever given to her of any change likely to he made. Some of the people interested in the school determined to enlarge the schools, or build new schools, and accordingly a new place was built, and it was then practically that Miss Tait for the first time ever heard of any change purported to be made in her position. On January 29 of the present year,she received a letter from Mr. Andrews, the curate of Haydock, who had succeeded to the position of secretary,informing her that the transfer of the infants in the company's school to the new schools would take place on the following Monday. She was also informed that her position would he the same as in the company's school with regard to the salary, and also her share of the Government grant, hut she was further informed that Miss Sawyer had been appointed headmistress, and Mr. Andrews said that he - had no doubt that with Miss Sawyer as headmistress and herself as second mistress the school would do well. Miss Tait, very properly took exception to that, as it was not what she had been engaged for, and no pre- vious intimation had been given to her of the change. It seemed to him (Mr. Jordan) that this was only a ruse on the part of the defendants to keep her for some time, and then get rid of her, and leave the man- agement of the school to the other lady who had been appointed head mistress. Miss Tait would tell his , Honour that this action of the school managers would damage her in-petting another situation, as the Educa- tion Department would not take her as having been head mistress telt word(' only regard her as a certificated assistant mistrees. Some correspondence took place,and Miss Tait Wl3 informed that unless she agreed to the offer of the managers she must not take any part in the work of the school. Miss Tait refusedond he contended that as he was enaaged as h-ad mistress, in refusing to take the position of assistant mistress, she was entitled to the claim she had made out. His Honour: Has she been dismissed ? Mr.Jorrian said that he held that the fact that his client was not allowed tn come to the school as head mistress was practically a dismissal. At the time, Miss Tait was engaged, the people who engaged her were Noneonform- i.ra, and Miss Tait he believed was a Nonconformist. Now, the vicar and curate wished to have the schools in their own hands, and he suggested that this had some- thing to do with the present affair. His Honour said that it would be better to keep that nut. Mr. Jordan agreed and then called Miss Tait, who said that she was a certificated schoolmistress. She enreeiroreted her counsel's statement.—Cross-examined by Mr. Allen, she said she was infant mistress. Mr. Jarmar was head master of the rest of the school, hut she received no orders with regard to the school from him. The sehonl was not condemned, but owing to straitness rsf aeeommedetien, the new schools were built. The excellent grant had never been given to the schools owingwhet h et thatw n ilid nacoctommemanodattitiaotn. the All Asellheen ael gawinaeasked con- demned, hut hi; Honour said that there was a difference court. ordering but ui tt he to between enl a r enlarged. condemnation cnn rie mo of tehd hoc hcnoniimatnya could not enlarge it. (Laughter.) John jacprer, master of the mixed department of the schools at Blackhrookssaid that he had been subpoenaed. The department over which he Was master contained no infants. and the appointment of the infant mistress was .PP3rate thine altogether. Miss Tait had had full con- trol of her department. The ordinary notice in termin- ating engagements in conneetion with school masters and school mistresses was three months.--Cross- examined : fie had never considered himself head master of the whole school. When returns were made to the Edtmetinn Department he always signed as prin- elnil of the mixed department. and Miss Tait as princi- pal of the infants' denartment. He did not know of any raeee where a month' e notice had been given to teachers but he had known even assistants to have a three months' notice. Mr. Allen for the defense submitted that there was no wronefril di:missal but the plaintiff had left the school herself voluntarily, His Honour Not according to the correspondence. Mr. Allen oaid that it would he necessary to go.further lot , the case. The school in question was.tmilt some years 3 ,() by the late Mr. Josenh Evans and at that time as now consisted of a mixed and infante' department. The population had increased tremendously and at the commencement of 1893 it Was found necessary to enlarge the school. When the transfer was made a meeting of the'ineira gers Was held, and it was considered that as the exeellent grant had never been earned, Miss Tait wes not competent to undertake the duties of school- mistress in a larger arhent,l nd consequently Miss Sawyer wa• eneeeed, and Miss Tait Was placed as assistant mistress with the same salary as aforetime. He con- tended that she was never head mistress but Was under the dirreeion of Mr. Jagger. His, Honour said that the evidence was entirely against that. It had been proved that Miss Tait's posi- tion in the school Was an independent one. He must say that if everything that Mr. Allen had said was proved he must still hold that there had been wrongful dismissel. • Mr. Allen then proceeded to deal with the question of damages, and held that a month's notice was sufficient.

He then called W. S. Barrett, th enmmorcial manager for Richard Evans Rnd Co., and correspondent for Blackbrook school. He said that he had been manager of various sr/Innis for 25 cpars, and he had known many cases where, .nlinnimaster or iniEtress had given or taken a

OAS AND WATER COMMITTEE.

This committee reported that Mr. J. Smith had been elected chairman of the committee for the ensuing year. A letter from Mr. Webb, dated 20th March, re water supply had been considered, and the committee resolved that Messrs. Smith, Barron, Stone, and MacMahon be a sub-committee to confer, and if necessary, seek an interview with Mr. Webb. A letter was read from Captain Royse re his deposit on gas. It was resolved that interest be allowed at 4 per cent, per annum, on all consumers' deposits, the same to date from Lady Day last. The carbonizing account for the past month was read, giving fuel used 304 tons 18 cwt. ; gas made 3,069,000 feet, being an average of 10,049 feet per ton of fuel used. The gas. manager re- . ..

ported the illuminating quality of the gas at 19 3 Candles that 18 drawn joints had been made good in the St. Helens-road between Alderson's Farm and Evans and Co.'s Crossing, and five drawn joints in Market-street, between St. John's Church and St. Helens-road, which has caused a very heavy leakage of gas. The water supply during the past month had been well maintained, but complaints had been made of its sandy nature ; the cause was explained by Mr. Emmett. The gas manager was instructed to try two wagons of Arley Nuts from Messrs. Fletcher, Burrows, and Co. It was also resolved that the street lamp lighting be continued through the summer months whln necessary.--Mr. Smith moved and Mr. Evans seconded the minutes which were passed unanimously.

THE OVEHSEERSHIP.

The Chairman said that he had received a petition with regard to the appointment of overseers for the township of Newton.—Mr. Smith : How many yards long ?—The Chairman : M long as myself. The Chair- man then proceeded to read the petition whi6h ran as follows : " We, the undersigned ratepayers of Newton- in-Makerfield. wish to express our disapproval of your action —Oh !)—in disregarding one of our nominations for overseers, on the plea that you received no written notification thereof. Further, we deem your action, as violating the spirit of the Local Government Act, 1894, and respectfully ask that you will amicately settle the difficulty by appointing our remaining nominee as an additional overseer. Your petitioners will over pray, etc," Then followed 300 er 400 names.—Mr. Smith : I move that this insulting document lie on the table.—The Chairman said that he had been asked by a deputation to present that petition, and he considered it his duty to do so, but it was net to he taken as indicating his opinion as to the merits of the case at all. He only undertook to present it before the Council. He would ask the law clerk whether the statement that they were violating the spirit of the Local Government Act was correct.—Mr. Wersler said that he could not see in what , manner they could he said to he violating the spirit of the Local Government Act. Nothing they did in rela- tion to the appointment of overseers violsted the Act.— Mr. Simpson asked whether they could reopen the question.—Mr. Smith : We have appointed overseers for the year, and the discussion is therefore out of order. He pressed his motion.—Mr. Stone seconded Mr. Smith's motion that the neiltion lie on the table.—Mr. Gilmour a,ked whetherofficial notification had been sent to the Chairman with reference to the recommendation of the overseers for Newton-in-Makerfield —Mr. Brierley said that he could not say whether he had received offi- eial information or not.—The Rev. E. W. W. Kaye : Have we power to appoint three overseers ?—Mr.Smith: We are wasting time. We have already filled the places for this year.—Mr. Simpson : The petition is out of one works.—Mr. Ormand ; Mr. Simpson will have to prove that.—The Chairman said that Mr. Ormand was out of order.—Mr. Ormand : But Mr. Simpson made a statement which he will have to prove—The Chair- men: I did not catch Mr. Simpson's observation.—Mr. Ormand :But I did.—Mr. Gilmour was proceeding to contradict Mr. Simpson, when the Chairman called him to order.—Mr. Emmett thought Mr. Simpson ought to have been called to order, or else let Mr. Gilmour have a chance of reply. Mr. Simpson had made a statement which he could not prove unless he had seen the petition. Be would also venture to say that the statement was not correct.—Mr. Simpson The petition hag been going round the works ("What works ?") The Viaduct works, and it has net been seen outside.—Mr. Emmett said that Mr. Simpson should either be called upon to withdraw that statement or he prepired to prove it.—The Chair- man however closed the discussinn and put Mr. Smith's motion to the meeting. For it, there voted, the Chair- man, and Messrs. Collingwood, Wood, Stone, Simpson, Smith, Thompson, and Kaye ; against, Messrs. Emmett, MacMahen. Gilmour, Beatty, Evans, end Ormand. The mot,on was therefore passed by eight votes to six. Mr. Gilmmir asked who appointed the assistant over- seer.—Mr. Wnrsley replied that this was an appoint- ment of the Guardians. The Council then proceeded to discuss the new stand- ing orders, and the meeting afterwards terminated.

Dr. Brabazon and J. °reason, Alexander Atherton was charged with having acted as a pedlar, without a certificate, at Booth's Hill, Lymm.—Sergeant Washington proved the case.—The magistrates fined defendant Ss. including costs, or, in default, seven days' Imprisonment.

IKThe infirmary question is the most important theme at present before the Warrington Board of Goardians, and in fact has been for two or three years. It has, however, of late come to the fore a little more, owing to the infusion of new blood on the Board,and at the last meeting a further step was taken by appointing a committee of eight to go into the matter thoroughly, and report to the Board at the earliest convenience. There is thus, at length, something definite being done. The Guardians are, however, not unanimous upon the subject, so that it is not yet certain bow the matter will terminate.

MELANCHOLY FATALITY AT Li HIESTO WN.

A LADY DECAPITATED.

On Wednesday afternoon about one o'clock, Miss Jane Kerfoot, a maiden lady 54 years of age, who resided with her brother in Wargrave-road, met with her death in a shocking manner, at Earles- town station through being knocked down and run over by a train of empty coal wagons. It appears she left home shortly before one o'clock for the pur- pose of catching the 1-9 p.m. train to Warrington. She was !ming to the station by way of the curve footbridge, and the path leading to the station. As she was proceeding along the path an empty wagon train was coming. from the direction of Warring- ton to Ilaydork. Ile train being a long one, Miss Kerfoot doubtless thought she would get over the crossing before the train passed and thus save a little time. The driver saw her pass in front of the engine, and when on the crosAng it is supposed she became confused by the near approach of the engine, and be ore she had time to turn back or go forward, she was knocked down and run over, her head and her left foot being severed. The train was stopped, and the body which had been carried on to the Liverpool main line was taken from under the wheels, and after being placed on the ambulance litter, was removed home.

===== 0002601_18950511_art0178.txt | included=true | included =====
THE INQUEST.

An inquiry into the circumstances was held at the Railway Inn, Earlestown, on;Thursday afternoon by Mr. S. Brighouse, coroner. Mr. T. Waywell was foreman of the jury. Mr. Fenna from the solicitor's office, Lime- street station, was present on behalf of the London and North Western Railway Company ; Messrs. E. and J. George and J. Lath4tn watched the proceedings in the interest of Messrs. R. Evans and Company. Mr. George Kerfoot said the deceased was his sister, and resid,cl with him in Wargrave-road. Her age last birthday was 154 years. He last saw her alive at 7.40 on the morning of the accident, He knew she was going by train dur- ing the day to Warrington, and he heard shortly after the occurence that she had been killed. Previous to her death she was in the best of health, had good hearing and eyesight. She ha l lived at the above address since September last, and had used the station frequently. When going from the Wargrave-road they had to go to the station to book. He had often seen coal trains pacing over the crossing at the end of the platform, and he con- sidered it a dangerous place.—? C. J.T. Schofield said he was a constable stationed at Wargrave. He was well acquainted with the deceased, and saw her leaving home about 12.50 on Wednesday, just as he was leaving his house. He walked down with her talking about the

weather, and they crossed the curve bridie together. After getting over the bridge she hurried on and got about 20 yards in front of him. When she got within a yard or two of the four foot, he could see the train corn- ing,,,and it would be about 40 yards down the siding frem Warrington. There were two young ladies comng off the platform at the same time, to get to the curve. Re shouted to the deceased and the young ladies, to look out. The latter stepped back, but the deceased stood in the middle of the four foot and was knocked down. She looked in the direction of the train, but did not appear to be able to move either one way or the other. The train would then be about a dozen yards from her. be afterwards found her on the main line, dead. fie did not hear the engine whistle. The train was stopped when about a dezen wagons had passed over the deceased, and the fireman assisted him to pull her from under the wagons.—ln answer to the Coroner, witness said all persons goir.ct from the curve either Warrington or Newton way, mu, tgo over the crossing, and if they came from Wargrave they would have to pass over it twice.—Mr. Latham said about 20 trains per day passed o,ter the crossing.—Witness, continuing, said he had never seen any precautions taken by the Company to protect persons passing and repassing over the crossing. When trains were passing over, the signalman had to stand in the street, to protect one side of the cross- ing in the street, the other side being blocked by a large gate. When he opened the gate he had to take himself further away from the scene of the accident. -The Coroner Have you seen any of the official come down from the booking office and stand at the crossing when a coal train was passing ? No.—By Mr. Fenna : Witness would not swear positively that there was no whistle. The signalman was too far away to be

of any use protecting people at the crossing. He saw the train coming and deceased could also see it, there was nothing t o stop the view.—By a juryman : If he had not railed to the two young ladies he thought it qui*e likely they might have been caught by the train. —John Bite, the driver of the coal train, said he was _ . .

about 10 yards from the decf aged when he first saw her. Phe was in the four foot way, right in front of the en- gine. He was travelling about four miles an hour. He blew the whistle the moment he saw her making an attempt to cross,and then shut off steam. /t was cus- tomary to blow the whistle when passing over the cros- sing.—Mr. Young, stationmaster, said he had been at I Earlestown 31 years. He should say about 100 people went aver the crossing every day, and about 40 or 50 at night when it was dark. The Coroner What precautions were taken to protect , people going to and from the curve ? None whatever. This state of things had been in existence ever since he came to Earlestown,and there had never been an a,•cident at that snot before, but there had been miraculous escan, s.—Mr. Penna said if they put a bridge over this crossing it would have to be blocked at both ends.—The Coroner said he did not see why itshould not be blocked the same as they had blocked the Wargrave end of the curve bridge.—After a short consultation the jury returned a verdict of " Accidental death " and attached no blame to any of the Company's servants, but added a presentment that the Company should be strongly urged to make some provision for the protection of the public."

===== 0002601_18950511_art0179.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BAD OLD TIMES.

LECTURE AT EARLESTOWN. On Monday evening at the Baptist Chapel, Earles- town, the Rev. C. F. Aked. of Liverpool, gave a lecture on" The Bad Old Times," in aid of the church funds. Mr. T. Ormand presided, and there was a large audience. The Lecturer said they often heard people speak in praisai of the good old times, but the phrase was dis- tinctly unscientific and untrue. Whereas every nation in the world had put its golden age right back in the distant past the children of Israel always put their golden age in front. They were always reaching for- ward in search of the happy time which was to come, and through the darkness of captivity, in exile amid suffering and in wane, the people of Israel were still dreaming, hoping, longing for the goad time that was yet to come. For us to suppose the good old times are oast, and that the future is to be the bad times, was to fall below he level of Judaism, and below the level of 'Christianity itself. To talk about the good old times was unscientific. It was a vanishing quantity ;it was a" will o' the wisp," and the more they looked the less likely it was they would find when all these good things took place. As compared with the age in which we live they were had old times. He alluded to the times when people had to make a fire with the aid of a flint and steel. In the single art of getting a light,we had moNed further in 50 Years than the whole race of men had moved in 5,000 years before. Among the peonle of both classes, high and low,there had been a growth of deli- cacy, and refinement of morals and manners. Men had been more conspicuously and more ridiculcmsly the slaves of fashion than women had been. Whatever might have been the follies of women,the follies of men had been more numerous and more grotesque. There was that grotesque and hideous piece of machinery, the tall hat, and the high collar. There was a good deal of the dress of the present day they could not respect, hut when they came to the dress of the past good heavens ; think of the wigs. Ile spoke of Dr. John- son, who, he said, always carried in his gold headed cane a disinfectant, and this he did, because the houses were so dirty in the bad old times. The dress and hat gear of the women was formerly just as wonderful as the wigs. In about 1700 it was the fashion fea the women to wear their hair about 4 feet high, and when they got it up, they kept it up for about three months. But it was safe to say that we had moved a little on the matter of dress since the good old times. In 1842 when the Duke of Wellington was approached by the S.E Rail- , way Company for Parliamentary assistance in improving I the 3rd class passenger accommodation, he said,the pro- pnsition to improve the 3rd class accommodation would only be to encourage the lower orders to go wandering aimlessly about the country, and impede the public ser- vice. Mr. Aired then went on to allude to the Corn taws the empleyment of child labour,and the conditions which produced crime, and of the county magistrates he said that of all the arrogant, insolent, muddle headed, lop- sided tribunals ever called into existence in a civilised world the English bench of county magistrates was the worst.

-A.-;ote of thanks to the lecturer and chairman was accorded at the close.
